\displaystyle\hskip 51.7479pt=\lvert L_{1}\cup\{(z_{L,i}\oplus k_{2}\oplus k_{1},y_{i}\oplus k_{3})\}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{1}}\lvert R_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{1}}\lvert L_{2}\setminus\{(x_{i},y_{i})\}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{2}}\lvert R_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{2}}\lvert\mathbf{z}\rangle_{\mathsf{Z}}\lvert\mathbf{k}\rangle_{\mathsf{K}}.

(15)

– For
(x,y)\in L_{2,\sf target}^{(k_{2},\vec{z}_{L})}
, let
i\in[|L_{2}|]
be the index such that
y
is the
i
-th largest element in
\imaginary(L_{2})
, denoted by
y_{i}
, and let
x_{i}
be the unique value satisfying
(x_{i},y_{i})\in L_{2}
. Then
x=z_{L,i}\oplus k_{2}
and

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}\lvert L^{(k_{1},k_{3})}_{1}\cup L_{2}^{(k_{2},\vec{z}_{L})}\setminus\{(x,y)\}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}}\lvert R^{(k_{1},k_{3})}_{1}\cup R_{2}^{(k_{2},\vec{z}_{R})}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}}\lvert\mathbf{k}\rangle_{\mathsf{K}}

\displaystyle\hskip 51.7479pt=\lvert L_{1}\cup\{(x_{i}\oplus k_{1},z_{L,i}\oplus k_{3})\}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{1}}\lvert R_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{1}}\lvert L_{2}\setminus\{(x_{i},y_{i})\}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{2}}\lvert R_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{2}}\lvert\mathbf{z}\rangle_{\mathsf{Z}}\lvert\mathbf{k}\rangle_{\mathsf{K}}.

(16)

Proof.

The proof consists of two steps. First, we establish decodability. Then, by reusing the same argument, we prove the remaining part of the lemma.

Step 1:
Fix the tuple. Let
L
be shorthand for
L^{(k_{1},k_{3})}_{1}\cup L_{2}^{(k_{2},\vec{z}_{L})}
. Recall
Definitions

4.9
and
4.10
. We first show that
G^{\ell}_{L,k_{2}}
is decomposable. The first and second conditions together imply that each vertex in
G^{\ell}_{L,k_{2}}
has a distinct label. Next, note that from the definition of augmented relation
L_{2}^{(k_{2},\vec{z}_{L})}
(see
Definition

4.11
), there are at least
|L_{2}|
edges in
G^{\ell}_{L,k_{2}}
. Together with the third condition, they imply that these
|L_{2}|
edges form a perfect matching of vertices in
L_{2}^{(k_{2},\vec{z}_{L})}
. Thus,
G^{\ell}_{L,k_{2}}
is decomposable. In particular, we have

\displaystyle V_{\sf isolate}(G^{\ell}_{L,k_{2}})=L_{1}^{(k_{1},k_{3})}

(17)

\displaystyle V_{\sf source}(G^{\ell}_{L,k_{2}})=\{(x,y)\in L_{2}^{(k_{2},\vec{z}_{L})}\colon y\in\{z_{L,1},\ldots,z_{L,|L_{2}|}\}\}

\displaystyle V_{\sf target}(G^{\ell}_{L,k_{2}})=L_{2}^{(k_{2},\vec{z}_{L})}\setminus V_{\sf source}(G^{\ell}_{L,k_{2}})

By symmetry, we can show that
G^{r}_{R,k_{2}}
is also decomposable, so the input will pass Step 1 of
\operatorname{Dec}
in
Definition

4.13
. Next, the first condition ensures the image of
V_{\sf target}(G^{\ell}_{L,k_{2}})
is
{\mathcal{I}}
-distinct and
V_{\sf target}(G^{r}_{L,k_{2}})
is
{\mathcal{D}}
-distinct, so the input will pass Step 2. By inspection, after Step 4, we obtain
L_{\sf isolate}=L_{1},R_{\sf isolate}=R_{1},L_{\sf pair}=L_{2},R_{\sf pair}=R_{2},\vec{m}_{L}=\vec{z}_{L},\vec{m}_{L}=\vec{z}_{R}
. Finally, the first condition ensures that the input passes Steps 5 and 6. This shows that the tuple is decodable and
Equation

13
.

Step 2:
In the view of relation-key induced graphs, deleting
v=(x,y)
from
L
is equivalently as deleting
v
from
G^{\ell}_{k_{2},L}
, together with all edges incident to
v
, that is, all edges either entering or leaving
v
. We denote this resulting graph by
G^{\ell}_{k_{2},L}\!-\!v
. Suppose
v\in V_{\sf isolate}(G^{\ell}_{L,k_{2}})
and
L_{1}
satisfies
L_{1}=L^{\prime}_{1}\cup\{(x\oplus k_{1},y\oplus k_{3})\}
for some
L^{\prime}_{1}
. From
Equation

17
, it is not hard to verify that

\displaystyle V_{\sf isolate}(G^{\ell}_{L,k_{2}}\!-\!v)=(L^{\prime}_{1})^{(k_{1},k_{3})}

\displaystyle V_{\sf source}(G^{\ell}_{L,k_{2}}\!-\!v)=\{(x,y)\in L_{2}^{(k_{2},\vec{z}_{L})}\colon y\in\{z_{L,1},\ldots,z_{L,|L_{2}|}\}\}

\displaystyle V_{\sf target}(G^{\ell}_{L,k_{2}}\!-\!v)=L_{2}^{(k_{2},\vec{z}_{L})}\setminus V_{\sf source}(G^{\ell}_{L,k_{2}})

and they pass Steps 5 and 6 of
\operatorname{Dec}
. Thus, we proved
Equation

14
.

When
v\in V_{\sf source}(G^{\ell}_{L,k_{2}})
. Suppose
L_{2}=\{(x_{i},y_{i})\}_{i\in[|L_{2}|]}
and
L_{2}^{(k_{2},\vec{z}_{L})}=\{(x_{i},z_{i}),(z_{i}\oplus k_{2},y_{i})\}_{i\in[|L_{2}|]}
. In the former case,
(x,y)=(x_{i^{*}},z_{i^{*}})
for some
i^{*}\in[|L_{2}|]
. As a result, the vertex
(z_{i^{*}}\oplus k_{2},y_{i^{*}})
in the graph
G^{\ell}_{L,k_{2}}\!-\!v
becomes “unpaired”. We may view
(z_{i^{*}}\oplus k_{2},y_{i^{*}})
being assigned to the isolated vertices. Namely,

\displaystyle V_{\sf isolate}(G^{\ell}_{L,k_{2}}\!-\!v)=(L_{1}\cup\{(z_{i^{*}}\oplus k_{2}\oplus k_{1},y_{i^{*}}\oplus k_{3})\})^{(k_{1},k_{3})}

\displaystyle V_{\sf source}(G^{\ell}_{L,k_{2}}\!-\!v)=\{(x_{i},z_{i})\}_{i\neq i^{*}}

\displaystyle V_{\sf target}(G^{\ell}_{L,k_{2}}\!-\!v)=\{(z_{i}\oplus k_{2},y_{i})\}_{i\neq i^{*}}

At first sight, the resulting graph remains decomposable. However, a caveat is that the newly added element
(z_{i^{*}}\oplus k_{2}\oplus k_{1},\,y_{i^{*}}\oplus k_{3})
may coincide with an element of
L_{1}
, which would cause Step 5 of the decoder
\operatorname{Dec}
(
Definition

4.13
) to fail. Fortunately, Item 2 prevent this event from happening. This proves
Equation

15
.

Finally, if
v\in V_{\sf target}(G^{\ell}_{L,k_{2}})
, we have
(x,y)=(z_{i^{*}}\oplus k_{2},y_{i^{*}})
for some
i^{*}\in[|L_{2}|]
and

\displaystyle V_{\sf isolate}(G^{\ell}_{L,k_{2}}\!-\!v)=(L_{1}\cup\{(x_{i^{*}}\oplus k_{1},z_{i^{*}}\oplus k_{3})\})^{(k_{1},k_{3})}

\displaystyle V_{\sf source}(G^{\ell}_{L,k_{2}}\!-\!v)=\{(x_{i},z_{i})\}_{i\neq i^{*}}

\displaystyle V_{\sf target}(G^{\ell}_{L,k_{2}}\!-\!v)=\{(z_{i}\oplus k_{2},y_{i})\}_{i\neq i^{*}}.

Similarly, Item 2 prevent this event from happening. This proves
Equation

16
.
∎

4.5
Good Tuples and Their Combinatorial Properties

In the following, we define good tuples.

Definition 4.19

(Good tuples)
.

For any
L_{1},L_{2}\in{\mathcal{R}}^{{\mathcal{I}}\text{-}\mathrm{dist}}
,
R_{1},R_{2}\in{\mathcal{R}}^{{\mathcal{D}}\text{-}\mathrm{dist}}
, let
\mathsf{G}\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1},L_{2}\\
R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}\right)
denote the set of
5
-tuples
(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},\vec{z}_{L},\vec{z}_{R})\in[N]\times[N]\times[N]\times[N]^{|L_{2}|}\times[N]^{|R_{2}|}
satisfying all the following conditions:
15
15

15

Recall that for a set
A\subseteq\{0,1\}^{n}
and a string
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, we denote
A\oplus x\coloneqq\{a\oplus x:a\in A\}
.

1.

k_{1}\notin\Big(\operatorname{Dom}(L_{1})\oplus\operatorname{Dom}(L_{2})\Big)\cup\Big(\operatorname{Dom}(R_{1})\oplus\operatorname{Dom}(R_{2})\Big)

2.

k_{2}\notin\Bigg(\bigg(\Big(\operatorname{Dom}(L_{1})\oplus k_{1}\Big)\cup\operatorname{Dom}(L_{2})\bigg)\oplus\bigg(\Big(\imaginary(L_{1})\oplus k_{3}\Big)\cup\imaginary(L_{2})\bigg)\Bigg)\\
\cup\Bigg(\bigg(\Big(\operatorname{Dom}(R_{1})\oplus k_{1}\Big)\cup\operatorname{Dom}(R_{2})\bigg)\oplus\bigg(\Big(\imaginary(R_{1})\oplus k_{3}\Big)\cup\imaginary(R_{2})\bigg)\Bigg)

3.

k_{3}\notin\Big(\imaginary(L_{1})\oplus\imaginary(L_{2})\Big)\cup\Big(\imaginary(R_{1})\oplus\imaginary(R_{2})\Big)

4.

\vec{z}_{L}\in[N]^{|L_{2}|}_{\mathrm{dist}}
and
\vec{z}_{R}\in[N]^{|R_{2}|}_{\mathrm{dist}}

5.

\{\vec{z}_{L}\}
and
\Big(\imaginary(L_{1})\oplus k_{3}\Big)\cup\imaginary(L_{2})\cup\Bigg(\bigg(\Big(\operatorname{Dom}(L_{1})\oplus k_{1}\Big)\cup\operatorname{Dom}(L_{2})\bigg)\oplus k_{2}\Bigg)
are disjoint.

6.

\{\vec{z}_{R}\}
and
\Big(\imaginary(R_{1})\oplus k_{3}\Big)\cup\imaginary(R_{2})\cup\Bigg(\bigg(\Big(\operatorname{Dom}(R_{1})\oplus k_{1}\Big)\cup\operatorname{Dom}(R_{2})\bigg)\oplus k_{2}\Bigg)
are disjoint.

Lemma 4.20
.

For any
L_{1},L_{2}\in{\mathcal{R}}^{{\mathcal{I}}\text{-}\mathrm{dist}}
,
R_{1},R_{2}\in{\mathcal{R}}^{{\mathcal{D}}\text{-}\mathrm{dist}}
, every tuple in
\mathsf{G}\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1},L_{2}\\
R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}\right)
satisfies all conditions in
Lemma

4.18
and is therefore robustly decodable.

The proof proceeds by a direct verification of all conditions and is given in
Appendix

B
.

Remark 4.21
.

Indeed, one could have alternative definitions for good tuples. In particular, we only require them to be robustly decodable and satisfy all properties introduced in the following.

Enumerating good tuples.
Here we describe how to enumerate tuples from
\mathsf{G}\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1},L_{2}\\
R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}\right)
. First, for any
L_{1},L_{2}\in{\mathcal{R}}^{{\mathcal{I}}\text{-}\mathrm{dist}}
and
R_{1},R_{2}\in{\mathcal{R}}^{{\mathcal{D}}\text{-}\mathrm{dist}}
, we define the sets
{\mathcal{B}}_{1}(L_{1},L_{2},R_{1},R_{2})
and
{\mathcal{B}}_{3}(L_{1},L_{2},R_{1},R_{2})
, which can be viewed as “bad
k_{1}
and
k_{3}
”:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{B}}_{1}(L_{1},L_{2},R_{1},R_{2})\coloneqq\big(\operatorname{Dom}(L_{1})\oplus\operatorname{Dom}(L_{2})\big)\cup\big(\operatorname{Dom}(R_{1})\oplus\operatorname{Dom}(R_{2})\big),

\displaystyle{\mathcal{B}}_{3}(L_{1},L_{2},R_{1},R_{2})\coloneqq\big(\imaginary(L_{1})\oplus\imaginary(L_{2})\big)\cup\big(\imaginary(R_{1})\oplus\imaginary(R_{2})\big).

Next, for any
L_{1},L_{2}\in{\mathcal{R}}^{{\mathcal{I}}\text{-}\mathrm{dist}},R_{1},R_{2}\in{\mathcal{R}}^{{\mathcal{D}}\text{-}\mathrm{dist}}
, and “good
(k_{1},k_{3})
” pair, namely,
k_{1}\notin{\mathcal{B}}_{1}(L_{1},L_{2},R_{1},R_{2})
and
k_{3}\notin{\mathcal{B}}_{3}(L_{1},L_{2},R_{1},R_{2})
, define the set
{\mathcal{B}}_{2}(L_{1},L_{2},R_{1},R_{2},k_{1},k_{3})
consisting of “bad
k_{2}
”:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{B}}_{2}(L_{1},L_{2},R_{1},R_{2},k_{1},k_{3})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\Bigg(\bigg(\Big(\operatorname{Dom}(L_{1})\oplus k_{1}\Big)\cup\operatorname{Dom}(L_{2})\bigg)\oplus\bigg(\Big(\imaginary(L_{1})\oplus k_{3}\Big)\cup\imaginary(L_{2})\bigg)\Bigg)

\displaystyle\qquad\cup\Bigg(\bigg(\Big(\operatorname{Dom}(R_{1})\oplus k_{1}\Big)\cup\operatorname{Dom}(R_{2})\bigg)\oplus\bigg(\Big(\imaginary(R_{1})\oplus k_{3}\Big)\cup\imaginary(R_{2})\bigg)\Bigg).

Finally, for any
L_{1},L_{2}\in{\mathcal{R}}^{{\mathcal{I}}\text{-}\mathrm{dist}},R_{1},R_{2}\in{\mathcal{R}}^{{\mathcal{D}}\text{-}\mathrm{dist}}
and “good
(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})
” such that
k_{1}\notin{\mathcal{B}}_{1}(L_{1},L_{2},R_{1},R_{2})
,
k_{3}\notin{\mathcal{B}}_{3}(L_{1},L_{2},R_{1},R_{2})
, and
k_{2}\notin{\mathcal{B}}_{2}(L_{1},L_{2},R_{1},R_{2},k_{1},k_{3})
, define the sets consisting of “bad
\vec{z}_{L}
and
\vec{z}_{R}
” as

\displaystyle{\mathcal{B}}_{L}\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1},L_{2},\\
R_{1},R_{2},\\
k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}\end{subarray}\right)\coloneqq\Bigg\{\vec{z}_{L}\in[N]^{|L_{2}|}:\vec{z}_{L}\notin[N]^{|L_{2}|}_{\mathrm{dist}}

\displaystyle\qquad\lor\ \exists i\in[|L_{2}|]\ \text{s.t.}\ z_{L,i}\in\Big(\imaginary(L_{1})\oplus k_{3}\Big)\cup\imaginary(L_{2})\cup\Bigg(\bigg(\Big(\operatorname{Dom}(L_{1})\oplus k_{1}\Big)\cup\operatorname{Dom}(L_{2})\bigg)\oplus k_{2}\Bigg)\Bigg\},

\displaystyle{\mathcal{B}}_{R}\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1},L_{2},\\
R_{1},R_{2},\\
k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}\end{subarray}\right)\coloneqq\Bigg\{\vec{z}_{R}\in[N]^{|R_{2}|}:\vec{z}_{R}\notin[N]^{|R_{2}|}_{\mathrm{dist}}

\displaystyle\qquad\lor\ \exists i\in[|R_{2}|]\ \text{s.t.}\ z_{R,i}\in\Big(\imaginary(R_{1})\oplus k_{3}\Big)\cup\imaginary(R_{2})\cup\Bigg(\bigg(\Big(\operatorname{Dom}(R_{1})\oplus k_{1}\Big)\cup\operatorname{Dom}(R_{2})\bigg)\oplus k_{2}\Bigg)\Bigg\}.

Therefore, one can enumerate elements in
\mathsf{G}\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1},L_{2}\\
R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}\right)
by first choosing
(k_{1},k_{3})
, then
k_{2}
second, and finally
(\vec{z}_{L},\vec{z}_{R})
, ensuring that each choice avoids being bad. In addition, if all
L_{1},L_{2},R_{1},R_{2}
are all of polynomial size, then most choices of
k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},\vec{z}_{L}
and
\vec{z}_{R}
are good. This is simply because the number of good keys they can eliminate is at most quadratic in the size of the relations.

Lemma 4.22
.

For any integer
t\geq 0
,
L_{1},L_{2}\in{\mathcal{R}}^{{\mathcal{I}}\text{-}\mathrm{dist}}_{\leq t}
,
R_{1},R_{2}\in{\mathcal{R}}^{{\mathcal{D}}\text{-}\mathrm{dist}}_{\leq t}
, the following holds.

1.

{\mathcal{B}}_{1}(L_{1},L_{2},R_{1},R_{2})
and
{\mathcal{B}}_{3}(L_{1},L_{2},R_{1},R_{2})
each occupy at most a
2t^{2}/N
fraction of the universe
[N]
.

2.

For any
k_{1}\notin{\mathcal{B}}_{1}(L_{1},L_{2},R_{1},R_{2})
and
k_{3}\notin{\mathcal{B}}_{3}(L_{1},L_{2},R_{1},R_{2})
, the set
{\mathcal{B}}_{2}(L_{1},L_{2},R_{1},R_{2},k_{1},k_{3})
occupies at most a
8t^{2}/N
fraction of the universe
[N]
.

3.

For any
k_{1}\notin{\mathcal{B}}_{1}(L_{1},L_{2},R_{1},R_{2})
,
k_{3}\notin{\mathcal{B}}_{3}(L_{1},L_{2},R_{1},R_{2})
, and
k_{2}\notin{\mathcal{B}}_{2}(L_{1},L_{2},R_{1},R_{2},k_{1},k_{3})
, the sets
{\mathcal{B}}_{L}\!\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1},L_{2}\\
R_{1},R_{2}\\
k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}\end{subarray}\right)
and
{\mathcal{B}}_{R}\!\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1},L_{2}\\
R_{1},R_{2}\\
k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}\end{subarray}\right)
each occupy at most a
5t^{2}/N
fraction of their respective universes,
[N]^{|L_{2}|}
and
[N]^{|R_{2}|}
.

As a corollary, all but a
22t^{2}/N
fraction of the elements in
[N]^{3}\times[N]^{|L_{2}|}\times[N]^{|R_{2}|}
are good tuples.

Proof.

Notice that for any sets
A,B
, the size of the set
A\oplus B
is at most
|A|\cdot|B|
. Thus, Item 1 follows. Items 2 and 3 can be proved in a similar manner.
∎

Good tuples satisfy the following property.

Lemma 4.23

(Monotonicity)
.

For any
x\in[N],L_{1},L_{2}\in{\mathcal{R}}^{{\mathcal{I}}\text{-}\mathrm{dist}}
,
R_{1},R_{2}\in{\mathcal{R}}^{{\mathcal{D}}\text{-}\mathrm{dist}}
, the following holds:

1.

There do not exist
(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},\vec{z}_{L},\vec{z}_{R})\notin\mathsf{G}\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1},L_{2}\\
R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}\right)
and
y\notin\imaginary(L_{1})
such that
(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},\vec{z}_{L},\vec{z}_{R})\in\mathsf{G}\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1}\cup\{(x,y)\},L_{2}\\
R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}\right)
.

2.

There do not exist
(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},\vec{z}_{L},\vec{z}_{R})\notin\mathsf{G}\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1},L_{2}\\
R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}\right),z\in[N]
, and
y\notin\imaginary(L_{2})
such that
(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},\allowbreak\vec{z}_{L}^{\,(i\leftarrow z)},\vec{z}_{R})\in\mathsf{G}\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1},L_{2}\cup\{(x,y)\}\\
R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}\right)
, where
i
is the index such that
y
is the
i
-th largest element in
\imaginary(L_{2})\cup\{y\}
and
\vec{z}_{L}^{\,(i\leftarrow z)}
denotes the vector obtained by inserting
z
into the
i
-th coordinate of
\vec{z}_{L}
and shifting all subsequent coordinates one position to the right.

As a corollary, for any
x\in[N],L_{1},L_{2}\in{\mathcal{R}}^{{\mathcal{I}}\text{-}\mathrm{dist}}
,
R_{1},R_{2}\in{\mathcal{R}}^{{\mathcal{D}}\text{-}\mathrm{dist}}
and
(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},\vec{z}_{L},\vec{z}_{R})\notin\mathsf{G}\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1},L_{2}\\
R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}\right)
, the following holds:

1.

For every
y\notin\imaginary(L_{1})
,
\mathsf{G}\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1}\cup\{(x,y)\},L_{2}\\
R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}\right)\subseteq\mathsf{G}\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1},L_{2}\\
R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}\right)
.

2.

For every
y\notin\imaginary(L_{2})
,
(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},\vec{z}_{L},\vec{z}_{R})\in\mathsf{G}\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1},L_{2}\cup\{(x,y)\}\\
R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}\right)
, it holds that
(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},\vec{z}_{L,-i},\vec{z}_{R})\in\mathsf{G}\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1},L_{2}\\
R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}\right)
, where
i
is the index such that
y
is the
i
-th largest element in
\imaginary(L_{2})\cup\{y\}
and
\vec{z}_{L,-i}
denotes the vector obtained by deleting its
i
-th coordinate and shifting all subsequent coordinates one position to the left.

Proof.

Since
(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},\vec{z}_{L},\vec{z}_{R})\notin\mathsf{G}\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1},L_{2}\\
R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}\right)
, some condition in
Definition

4.19
must be violated. To prove Item 1, simply note that the same condition remains violated after adding
(x,y)
to
L_{1}
. To prove Item 2, observe that the conditions in
Definition

4.19
are insensitive to the ordering of
\vec{z}_{L}
and
\vec{z}_{R}
. Thus, although inserting
z
into
\vec{z}_{L}
changes its size and ordering, the same condition remains violated.
∎

The following lemmas will be used in
Section

6
.

Lemma 4.24
.

For any
t\geq 0,x\in[N],L_{1},L_{2}\in{\mathcal{R}}^{{\mathcal{I}}\text{-}\mathrm{dist}}_{\leq t}
,
R_{1},R_{2}\in{\mathcal{R}}^{{\mathcal{D}}\text{-}\mathrm{dist}}_{\leq t}
, there are at most a
t(22t+4)/N
fraction of the elements
(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z})\in[N]^{3}\times[N]^{|L_{2}|}\times[N]^{|R_{2}|}
for which no
y\notin\imaginary(L_{1})
satisfies
(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z})\in\mathsf{G}\!\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1}\cup\{(x,y)\},L_{2}\\
R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}\right)
.

Proof.

Let us sample
(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z})
uniformly at random from
[N]^{3}\times[N]^{|L_{2}|}\times[N]^{|R_{2}|}
. Define the following events

•

\mathsf{Good}\colon(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z})\in\mathsf{G}\!\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1},L_{2}\\
R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}\right)

•

\mathsf{K}_{1}\colon k_{1}\in x\oplus\operatorname{Dom}(L_{2})

•

\mathsf{K}_{2}\colon k_{2}\in x\oplus k_{1}\oplus\bigg(\Big(\imaginary(L_{1})\oplus k_{3}\Big)\cup\imaginary(L_{2})\bigg)

•

\mathsf{Z}_{L}\colon\{\vec{z}_{L}\}\cap\{x\oplus k_{1}\oplus k_{2}\}\neq\varnothing

Suppose
\mathsf{Good}
occurs, and consider the items in
Definition

4.19
.
For there to be no
y
such that

(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z})\in\mathsf{G}\!\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1}\cup\{(x,y)\},L_{2}\\
R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}\right)
,
one of the conditions imposed by the addition of
x
to
\operatorname{Dom}(L_{1})
must be violated.
These correspond to the events
\mathsf{K}_{1}
,
\mathsf{K}_{2}
, and
\mathsf{Z}_{L}
.
Otherwise, there always exists some
y
such that

(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z})\in\mathsf{G}\!\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1}\cup\{(x,y)\},L_{2}\\
R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}\right)
.
Therefore, by the union bound, the fraction of such
(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z})
is at most

\Pr[\neg\mathsf{Good}]+\Pr[\mathsf{K}_{1}]+\Pr[\mathsf{K}_{2}]+\Pr[\mathsf{Z}_{L}]\;\leq\;\frac{22t^{2}}{N}+\frac{t}{N}+\frac{2t}{N}+\frac{t}{N}\;=\;\frac{t(22t+4)}{N},

where
\Pr[\mathsf{K}_{1}]
,
\Pr[\mathsf{K}_{2}]
, and
\Pr[\mathsf{Z}_{L}]
are bounded
by sampling
k_{1}
,
k_{2}
, and
\vec{z}_{L}
, respectively, at the end.
∎

Lemma 4.25
.

For any
t\geq 0,x\in[N]
,
L_{1},L_{2}\in{\mathcal{R}}^{{\mathcal{I}}\text{-}\mathrm{dist}}_{\leq t}
,

R_{1},R_{2}\in{\mathcal{R}}^{{\mathcal{D}}\text{-}\mathrm{dist}}_{\leq t}
,
\mathbf{k}\in[N]^{3}
,
\mathbf{z}\in[N]^{|L_{2}|}\times[N]^{|R_{2}|}
, if there exists
y\notin\imaginary(L_{1})
such that
(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z})\in\mathsf{G}\!\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1}\cup\{(x,y)\},L_{2}\\
R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}\right)
, then there are at most
4t
values of
y\notin\imaginary(L_{1})
such that
(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z})\notin\mathsf{G}\!\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1}\cup\{(x,y)\},L_{2}\\
R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}\right)
.

Proof.

Suppose there exists some
y\notin\imaginary(L_{1})
such that
(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z})\in\mathsf{G}\!\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1}\cup\{(x,y)\},L_{2}\\
R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}\right)
. According to the definition of good tuples,
(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z})
satisfies all conditions in
Definition

4.19
.
Now, suppose we attempt to vary
y
in such a way that
(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z})
violates one of the conditions. Since we are only modifying
\imaginary(L_{1}\cup\{(x,y)\})
, Items 1, 4, and 6 continue to hold. We now examine Item 2. If we replace
y
with a different value
y^{\prime}
, the additional imposed constraint is

\displaystyle k_{2}\notin\bigg(\big(\operatorname{Dom}(L_{1})\oplus k_{1}\big)\cup\operatorname{Dom}(L_{2})\bigg)\oplus y^{\prime}\oplus k_{3}.

Thus, in order for this condition to be violated, we must have

\displaystyle y^{\prime}\in\bigg(\big(\operatorname{Dom}(L_{1})\oplus k_{1}\big)\cup\operatorname{Dom}(L_{2})\bigg)\oplus k_{2}\oplus k_{3},

and there are at most
2t
such values of
y^{\prime}
. Similarly, there are at most
t
values of
y^{\prime}
that violate Item 3 and at most
t
values of
y^{\prime}
that violate Item 5.
Hence, the total number of such
y^{\prime}
is bounded by
4t
.
∎

Lemma 4.26
.

For any
t\geq 0,x\in[N]
,
L_{1},L_{2}\in{\mathcal{R}}^{{\mathcal{I}}\text{-}\mathrm{dist}}_{\leq t}
,

R_{1},R_{2}\in{\mathcal{R}}^{{\mathcal{D}}\text{-}\mathrm{dist}}_{\leq t}
, define the following two sets:

\displaystyle\Psi_{\phantom{x,}L_{1},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}\coloneqq\{(y,\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z})\colon(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z})\in\mathsf{G}\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1},L_{2},\\
R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}\right),y\notin\imaginary(L_{1})\cup\quantity( \Im(L_2^{(k_2,\vec{z}_L)}) \oplus k_3 )\}

\displaystyle\Phi_{x,L_{1},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}\coloneqq\{(y,\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z})\colon y\notin\imaginary(L_{1}),(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z})\in\mathsf{G}\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1}\cup\{(x,y)\},L_{2},\\
R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}\right)\}.

Then
\Psi_{\phantom{x,}L_{1},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}\supseteq\Phi_{x,L_{1},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}
and the size of their difference is at most
t(22t+8)N^{|L_{2}|+|R_{2}|+3}
.

Proof.

Consider elements in
\Phi_{x,L_{1},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}
. First, from
Lemma

4.20
and Items 1 and 2 in
Lemma

4.18
, we have

(L_{1}\cup\{(x,y)\})^{(k_{1},k_{3})}\in{\mathcal{R}}^{{\mathcal{I}}\text{-}\mathrm{dist}}\quad\text{and}\quad\imaginary\left((L_{1}\cup\{(x,y)\})^{(k_{1},k_{3})}\right)\cap\imaginary(L_{2}^{(k_{2},\vec{z}_{L})})=\varnothing,

which implies
y\notin\imaginary(L_{1})\cup\left(\imaginary(L_{2}^{(k_{2},\vec{z}_{L})})\oplus k_{3}\right)
. Then by monotonicity (Item 1 in
Lemma

4.23
), we have

\mathsf{G}\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1}\cup\{(x,y)\},L_{2},\\
R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}\right)\subseteq\mathsf{G}\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1},L_{2},\\
R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}\right).

This proves
\Psi_{\phantom{x,}L_{1},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}\supseteq\Phi_{x,L_{1},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}
.

Next, we bound the size of their difference. We denote by
\mathsf{BAD}
the set of

(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z})\in\mathsf{G}\!\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1},L_{2}\\
R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}\right)
for which no

y\notin\imaginary(L_{1})
satisfies

(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z})\in\mathsf{G}\!\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1}\cup\{(x,y)\},L_{2}\\
R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}\right)
.
By
Lemma

4.24
, we have

|\mathsf{BAD}|\leq t(22t+4)N^{|L_{2}|+|R_{2}|+2}.

Now, if we enumerate
(y,\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z})
in
\Psi_{\phantom{x,}L_{1},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}
, then one of the following holds:

1.

If
(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z})\in\mathsf{BAD}
, then every
y\notin\imaginary(L_{1})
satisfies

(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z})\notin\mathsf{G}\!\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1}\cup\{(x,y)\},L_{2}\\
R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}\right)
, which in turn implies
(y,\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z})\notin\Phi_{x,L_{1},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}
.

2.

If
(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z})\notin\mathsf{BAD}
, then by
Lemma

4.25

there are at most
4t
values of
y\notin\imaginary(L_{1})
such that

(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z})\notin\mathsf{G}\!\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1}\cup\{(x,y)\},L_{2}\\
R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}\right)
. All other
y
satisfy
(y,\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z})\in\Phi_{x,L_{1},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}
.

Therefore, the size of their difference is at most

\displaystyle|\mathsf{BAD}|\cdot N+N^{|L_{2}|+|R_{2}|+3}\cdot 4t\leq t(22t+8)N^{|L_{2}|+|R_{2}|+3}.

∎

The following lemmas will be used in
Section

7
.

Lemma 4.27
.

For any
t\geq 0,x\in[N],L_{1},L_{2}\in{\mathcal{R}}^{{\mathcal{I}}\text{-}\mathrm{dist}}_{\leq t}
,
R_{1},R_{2}\in{\mathcal{R}}^{{\mathcal{D}}\text{-}\mathrm{dist}}_{\leq t}
, there are at most a
(22t^{2}+10t+1)/N
fraction of elements
(z,\mathbf{k},\vec{z}_{L},\vec{z}_{R})\in[N]\times[N]^{3}\times[N]^{|L_{2}|}\times[N]^{|R_{2}|}
for which no
y\notin\imaginary(L_{2})
satisfies
(\mathbf{k},\vec{z}^{\,(i\leftarrow z)}_{L},\vec{z}_{R})\in\mathsf{G}\!\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1},L_{2}\cup\{(x,y)\}\\
R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}\right)
where
i
is the index such that
y\in_{i}\imaginary(L_{2})\cup\{y\}
.

Proof.

Let us sample
(z,\mathbf{k},\vec{z}_{L},\vec{z}_{R})
uniformly at random from
[N]\times[N]^{3}\times[N]^{|L_{2}|}\times[N]^{|R_{2}|}
. Define the following events

•

\mathsf{Good}\colon(\mathbf{k},\vec{z}_{L},\vec{z}_{R})\in\mathsf{G}\!\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1},L_{2}\\
R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}\right)

•

\mathsf{K}_{1}\colon k_{1}\in\operatorname{Dom}(L_{1})\oplus x

•

\mathsf{K}_{2}\colon k_{2}\in x\oplus\bigg(\Big(\imaginary(L_{1})\oplus k_{3}\Big)\cup\imaginary(L_{2})\bigg)

•

\mathsf{Z}_{L}\colon z\in\{\vec{z}_{L}\}

or

x\oplus k_{2}\in\{\vec{z}_{L}\}

or

x\in\{\vec{z}_{L}\}

•

\mathsf{Z}\colon z\in\Big(\imaginary(L_{1})\oplus k_{3}\Big)\cup\imaginary(L_{2})\cup\Bigg(\bigg(\Big(\operatorname{Dom}(L_{1})\oplus k_{1}\Big)\cup\operatorname{Dom}(L_{2})\bigg)\oplus k_{2}\Bigg)

or

z=x

Suppose
\mathsf{Good}
occurs, and consider the items in
Definition

4.19
. For there to be no
y
such that
(\mathbf{k},\vec{z}^{\,(i\leftarrow z)}_{L},\vec{z}_{R})\in\mathsf{G}\!\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1},L_{2}\cup\{(x,y)\}\\
R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}\right)
, one of the conditions imposed by the addition of (i)
x
to
\operatorname{Dom}(L_{1})
or (ii)
z
to
\vec{z}_{L}
must be violated. These correspond to the events
\mathsf{K}_{1}
,
\mathsf{K}_{2}
,
\mathsf{Z}_{L}
, and
\mathsf{Z}
. Otherwise, there always exists some
y
such that
(\mathbf{k},\vec{z}^{\,(i\leftarrow z)}_{L},\vec{z}_{R})\in\mathsf{G}\!\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1},L_{2}\cup\{(x,y)\}\\
R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}\right)
. Therefore, by the union bound, the fraction of such
(z,\mathbf{k},\vec{z}_{L},\vec{z}_{R})
is at most

\Pr[\neg\mathsf{Good}]+\Pr[\mathsf{K}_{1}]+\Pr[\mathsf{K}_{2}]+\Pr[\mathsf{Z}_{L}]+\Pr[\mathsf{Z}]\;\leq\;\frac{22t^{2}}{N}+\frac{t}{N}+\frac{2t}{N}+\frac{3t}{N}+\frac{4t+1}{N}\;=\;\frac{22t^{2}+10t+1}{N},

where
\Pr[\mathsf{K}_{1}]
,
\Pr[\mathsf{K}_{2}]
,
\Pr[\mathsf{Z}_{L}]
, and
\Pr[\mathsf{Z}]
are bounded by sampling
k_{1}
,
k_{2}
,
\vec{z}_{L}
, and
z
, respectively, at the end.
∎

Lemma 4.28
.

For any
t\geq 0,x\in[N]
,
L_{1},L_{2}\in{\mathcal{R}}^{{\mathcal{I}}\text{-}\mathrm{dist}}_{\leq t}
,

R_{1},R_{2}\in{\mathcal{R}}^{{\mathcal{D}}\text{-}\mathrm{dist}}_{\leq t}
,
\mathbf{k}\in[N]^{3}
,
\vec{z}_{L}\in[N]^{|L_{2}|-1}
,
z\in[N]
and
\vec{z}_{R}\in[N]^{|R_{2}|}
, if there exists
y\notin\imaginary(L_{2})
such that
(\mathbf{k},\vec{z}^{\,(i\leftarrow z)}_{L},\vec{z}_{R})\in\mathsf{G}\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1},L_{2}\cup\{(x,y)\}\\
R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}\right)
where
i
is the index such that
y\in_{i}\imaginary(L_{2})\cup\{y\}
, then there are at most
4t+1
values of
y\notin\imaginary(L_{2})
such that
(\mathbf{k},\vec{z}^{\,(i\leftarrow z)}_{L},\vec{z}_{R})\notin\mathsf{G}\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1},L_{2}\cup\{(x,y)\}\\
R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}\right)
where
i
is the index such that
y\in_{i}\imaginary(L_{2})\cup\{y\}
.

Proof.

Suppose there exists some
y\notin\imaginary(L_{2})
such that
(\mathbf{k},\vec{z}^{\,(i\leftarrow z)}_{L},\vec{z}_{R})\in\mathsf{G}\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1},L_{2}\cup\{(x,y)\}\\
R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}\right)
. According to the definition of good tuples,
(\mathbf{k},\vec{z}^{\,(i\leftarrow z)}_{L},\vec{z}_{R})
satisfies all conditions in
Definition

4.19
.
Now, suppose we attempt to vary
y
in such a way that
(\mathbf{k},\vec{z}^{\,(i\leftarrow z)}_{L},\vec{z}_{R})
violates one of the conditions. Notice that the index
i
might vary with the value of
y
because it is defined to be the index such that
y\in_{i}\imaginary(L_{2})\cup\{y\}
. Since we are only modifying
\imaginary(L_{2}\cup\{(x,y)\})
, and the conditions in
Definition

4.19
depend only on the set
\{\vec{z}^{\,(i\leftarrow z)}_{L}\}
rather than on the ordering of
\vec{z}^{\,(i\leftarrow z)}_{L}
, Items 1, 4, and 6 continue to hold. We can apply the same argument as in the proof of
Lemma

4.25
to show that the total number of such
y^{\prime}
is
4t+1
. This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 4.29
.

For any
x\in[N]
,
L_{1},L_{2}\in{\mathcal{R}}^{{\mathcal{I}}\text{-}\mathrm{dist}}_{\leq t}
,

R_{1},R_{2}\in{\mathcal{R}}^{{\mathcal{D}}\text{-}\mathrm{dist}}_{\leq t}
, define the following two sets:

\displaystyle\Psi_{\phantom{x,}L_{1},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}\coloneqq\{(y,z,\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z})\colon(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z})\in\mathsf{G}\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1},L_{2}\\
R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}\right),z,y\notin\imaginary(L_{1}^{(k_{1},k_{3})}\cup L_{2}^{(k_{2},\vec{z}_{L})}),y\neq z^{\prime}\}

\displaystyle\Phi_{x,L_{1},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}\coloneqq\{(y,z,\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z})\colon y\notin\imaginary(L_{2}),i\ \text{s.t.}\ y\,\in_{i}\,\imaginary(L_{2})\cup\{y\},(\mathbf{k},\vec{z}_{L}^{\,(i\leftarrow z)},\vec{z}_{R})\in\mathsf{G}\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1},L_{2}\cup\{(x,y)\}\\
R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}\right)\}.

Then
\Psi_{\phantom{x,}L_{1},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}\supseteq\Phi_{x,L_{1},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}
and the size of their difference is at most
(22t^{2}+14t+2)N^{|L_{2}|+|R_{2}|+4}
.

Proof.

Consider elements in
\Phi_{x,L_{1},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}
. First, from
Lemma

4.20
and Items 1 and 2 in
Lemma

4.18
, we have

(L_{2}\cup\{(x,y)\})^{(k_{2},\vec{z}_{L}^{\,(i\leftarrow z)})}\in{\mathcal{R}}^{{\mathcal{I}}\text{-}\mathrm{dist}}\quad\text{and}\quad\imaginary(L_{1}^{(k_{1},k_{3})})\cap\imaginary\left((L_{2}\cup\{(x,y)\})^{(k_{2},\vec{z}_{L}^{\,(i\leftarrow z)})}\right)=\varnothing,

which implies
z,y\notin\imaginary(L_{1}^{(k_{1},k_{3})}\cup L_{2}^{(k_{2},\vec{z}_{L})})\,\land\,y\neq z^{\prime}
. Then by monotonicity (Item 2 in
Lemma

4.23
), we have

(\mathbf{k},\vec{z}_{L}^{\,(i\leftarrow z)},\vec{z}_{R})\in\mathsf{G}\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1},L_{2}\cup\{(x,y)\}\\
R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}\right)\implies(\mathbf{k},\vec{z}_{L},\vec{z}_{R})\in\mathsf{G}\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1},L_{2}\\
R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}\right).

This proves
\Psi_{\phantom{x,}L_{1},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}\supseteq\Phi_{x,L_{1},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}
.

Next, we bound the size of their difference. We denote by
\mathsf{BAD}
the set of

(z,\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z})\in[N]\times\mathsf{G}\!\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1},L_{2}\\
R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}\right)
for which no
y\notin\imaginary(L_{2})
satisfies
(\mathbf{k},\vec{z}_{L}^{\,(i\leftarrow z)},\vec{z}_{R})\in\mathsf{G}\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1},L_{2}\cup\{(x,y)\}\\
R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}\right)
. By
Lemma

4.27
, we have

|\mathsf{BAD}|=(22t^{2}+10t+1)N^{|L_{2}|+|R_{2}|+3}.

Now, if we enumerate
(y,z,\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z})
in
\Psi_{\phantom{x,}L_{1},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}
, then one of the following holds:

1.

If
(z,\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z})\in\mathsf{BAD}
, then every
y\notin\imaginary(L_{2})
satisfies

(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z})\notin\mathsf{G}\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1},L_{2}\cup\{(x,y)\}\\
R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}\right)
, which in turn implies
(y,\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z})\notin\Phi_{x,L_{1},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}
.

2.

If
(z,\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z})\notin\mathsf{BAD}
, then by
Lemma

4.28
there are at most
4t+1
values of
y\notin\imaginary(L_{2})
for which
(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z})\notin\mathsf{G}\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1},L_{2}\cup\{(x,y)\}\\
R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}\right)
. All other
y
satisfy
(y,\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z})\in\Phi_{x,L_{1},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}
.

Therefore, the size of their difference is at most

\displaystyle|\mathsf{BAD}|\cdot N+N^{|L_{2}|+|R_{2}|+4}\cdot(4t+1)\leq(22t^{2}+14t+2)N^{|L_{2}|+|R_{2}|+4}.

∎

4.6
Defining the Approximate Isometry
{\mathcal{S}}

Before defining
{\mathcal{S}}
, which intuitively maps a view in hybrid
\mathbf{H}_{3}
to a uniform superposition of consistent views in hybrid
\mathbf{H}_{4}
, we first define the following operators, which mimic each step of enumerating good tuples in the previous subsection.

Definition 4.30

(Operator
{\mathcal{S}}_{k_{1},k_{3}}
)
.

Define the operator
{\mathcal{S}}_{k_{1},k_{3}}
such that for any
L_{1},L_{2}\in{\mathcal{R}}^{{\mathcal{I}}\text{-}\mathrm{dist}},R_{1},R_{2}\in{\mathcal{R}}^{{\mathcal{D}}\text{-}\mathrm{dist}}
,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{S}}_{k_{1},k_{3}}\colon\lvert L_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{1}}\lvert R_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{1}}\lvert L_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{2}}\lvert R_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{2}}\mapsto\frac{1}{\sqrt{N^{2}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}\notin{\mathcal{B}}_{1}(L_{1},L_{2},R_{1},R_{2})\\
k_{3}\notin{\mathcal{B}}_{3}(L_{1},L_{2},R_{1},R_{2})\end{subarray}}\lvert L_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{1}}\lvert R_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{1}}\lvert L_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{2}}\lvert R_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{2}}\lvert k_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{K}_{1}}\lvert k_{3}\rangle_{\mathsf{K}_{3}}.

(18)

Otherwise,
{\mathcal{S}}_{k_{1},k_{3}}
maps all the other basis vectors to the zero vector.

Definition 4.31

(Operator
{\mathcal{S}}_{k_{2}}
)
.

Define the operator
{\mathcal{S}}_{k_{2}}
such that for any
L_{1},L_{2}\in{\mathcal{R}}^{{\mathcal{I}}\text{-}\mathrm{dist}},R_{1},R_{2}\in{\mathcal{R}}^{{\mathcal{D}}\text{-}\mathrm{dist}},k_{1}\notin{\mathcal{B}}_{1}(L_{1},L_{2},R_{1},R_{2}),k_{3}\notin{\mathcal{B}}_{3}(L_{1},L_{2},R_{1},R_{2})
,

{\mathcal{S}}_{k_{2}}\colon\lvert L_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{1}}\lvert R_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{1}}\lvert L_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{2}}\lvert R_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{2}}\lvert k_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{K}_{1}}\lvert k_{3}\rangle_{\mathsf{K}_{3}}\mapsto\\
\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{k_{2}\notin{\mathcal{B}}_{2}(L_{1},L_{2},R_{1},R_{2},k_{1},k_{3})}\lvert L_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{1}}\lvert R_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{1}}\lvert L_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{2}}\lvert R_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{2}}\lvert k_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{K}_{1}}\lvert k_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{K}_{2}}\lvert k_{3}\rangle_{\mathsf{K}_{3}}.

(19)

Otherwise,
{\mathcal{S}}_{k_{2}}
maps all the other basis vectors to the zero vector.

Definition 4.32

(Operator
{\mathcal{S}}_{\vec{z}}
)
.

Define the operator
{\mathcal{S}}_{\vec{z}}
such that for any
L_{1},L_{2}\in{\mathcal{R}}^{{\mathcal{I}}\text{-}\mathrm{dist}},R_{1},R_{2}\in{\mathcal{R}}^{{\mathcal{D}}\text{-}\mathrm{dist}},k_{1}\notin{\mathcal{B}}_{1}(L_{1},L_{2},R_{1},R_{2}),k_{3}\notin{\mathcal{B}}_{3}(L_{1},L_{2},R_{1},R_{2}),k_{2}\notin{\mathcal{B}}_{2}(L_{1},L_{2},R_{1},R_{2},k_{1},k_{3})
,

{\mathcal{S}}_{\vec{z}}\colon\lvert L_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{1}}\lvert R_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{1}}\lvert L_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{2}}\lvert R_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{2}}\lvert k_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{K}_{1}}\lvert k_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{K}_{2}}\lvert k_{3}\rangle_{\mathsf{K}_{3}}\mapsto\\
\frac{1}{\sqrt{N^{|L_{2}|+|R_{2}|}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\vec{z}_{L}\notin{\mathcal{B}}_{L}\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1},L_{2},\\
R_{1},R_{2},\\
k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}\end{subarray}\right),\vec{z}_{R}\notin{\mathcal{B}}_{R}\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1},L_{2},\\
R_{1},R_{2},\\
k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}\end{subarray}\right)\end{subarray}}\lvert L_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{1}}\lvert R_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{1}}\lvert L_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{2}}\lvert R_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{2}}\lvert\vec{z}_{L}\rangle_{\mathsf{Z_{L}}}\lvert\vec{z}_{R}\rangle_{\mathsf{Z_{R}}}\lvert k_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{K}_{1}}\lvert k_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{K}_{2}}\lvert k_{3}\rangle_{\mathsf{K}_{3}}.

(20)

Otherwise,
{\mathcal{S}}_{\vec{z}}
maps all the other basis vectors to the zero vector.

Now, we define the operator
{\mathcal{S}}
.

Definition 4.33

(Operator
{\mathcal{S}}
)
.

Define the operator

\displaystyle{\mathcal{S}}\coloneqq{\mathcal{D}}^{\dagger}\cdot{\mathcal{S}}_{\vec{z}}\cdot{\mathcal{S}}_{k_{2}}\cdot{\mathcal{S}}_{k_{1},k_{3}}.

(21)

Note that
{\mathcal{S}}_{k_{1},k_{3}},{\mathcal{S}}_{k_{2}},{\mathcal{S}}_{\vec{z}}
are contractions, that is, their operator norms are all bounded
1
. Thus,
{\mathcal{S}}
is
not
a partial isometry. Importantly, the action of
{\mathcal{S}}
satisfies the following.

Lemma 4.34
.

For any
L_{1},L_{2}\in{\mathcal{R}}^{{\mathcal{I}}\text{-}\mathrm{dist}},R_{1},R_{2}\in{\mathcal{R}}^{{\mathcal{D}}\text{-}\mathrm{dist}}
,

{\mathcal{S}}\colon\lvert L_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{1}}\lvert R_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{1}}\lvert L_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{2}}\lvert R_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{2}}\mapsto\\
\frac{1}{\sqrt{N^{|L_{2}|+|R_{2}|+3}}}\sum_{(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},\vec{z}_{L},\vec{z}_{R})\in\mathsf{G}\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1},L_{2}\\
R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}\right)}\lvert L^{(k_{1},k_{3})}_{1}\cup L_{2}^{(k_{2},\vec{z}_{L})}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}}\lvert R^{(k_{1},k_{3})}_{1}\cup R_{2}^{(k_{2},\vec{z}_{R})}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}}\lvert k_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{K_{1}}}\lvert k_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{K_{2}}}\lvert k_{3}\rangle_{\mathsf{K_{3}}}.

Proof.

Recall
Definitions

4.30
,
4.31
and
4.32
, we have

\displaystyle\lvert L_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{1}}\lvert R_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{1}}\lvert L_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{2}}\lvert R_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{2}}\xmapsto{{\mathcal{S}}_{\vec{z}}\cdot{\mathcal{S}}_{k_{2}}\cdot{\mathcal{S}}_{k_{1},k_{3}}}

\displaystyle\hskip 68.99895pt\frac{1}{\sqrt{N^{|L_{2}|+|R_{2}|+3}}}\sum_{(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z})\in\mathsf{G}\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1},L_{2}\\
R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}\right)}\lvert L_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{1}}\lvert R_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{1}}\lvert L_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{2}}\lvert R_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{2}}\lvert\vec{z}_{L}\rangle_{\mathsf{Z_{L}}}\lvert\vec{z}_{R}\rangle_{\mathsf{Z_{R}}}\lvert k_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{K}_{1}}\lvert k_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{K}_{2}}\lvert k_{3}\rangle_{\mathsf{K}_{3}}.

From
Lemma

4.20
, every
(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z})\in\mathsf{G}\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1},L_{2}\\
R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}\right)
is decodable with respect to
(L_{1},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2})
. Thus,
(L_{1},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2},\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z})
is in
\operatorname{Supp}(\operatorname{Dec})
. Therefore, applying
{\mathcal{D}}^{\dagger}
is equivalently to applying
\operatorname{Enc}
, which the inverse of
\operatorname{Dec}
defined in the proof of
Lemma

4.14
. This completes the proof.
∎

The following lemma will be used in
Sections

6
and
7
.

Lemma 4.35
.

Let
\{{\mathcal{P}}_{\tau}\}_{\tau}
be a collection of sets where the index
\tau
ranges over
(y\in[N],L_{1}\in{\mathcal{R}}^{{\mathcal{I}}\text{-}\mathrm{dist}},R_{1}\in{\mathcal{R}}^{{\mathcal{D}}\text{-}\mathrm{dist}},L_{2}\in{\mathcal{R}}^{{\mathcal{I}}\text{-}\mathrm{dist}},R_{2}\in{\mathcal{R}}^{{\mathcal{D}}\text{-}\mathrm{dist}})
and
{\mathcal{P}}_{\tau}\subseteq[N]^{3}\times[N]^{|L_{2}|}\times[N]^{|R_{2}|}
. Define the operator

\displaystyle{\mathcal{S}}^{\bullet}

\displaystyle\colon\lvert y\rangle_{\mathsf{A}}\lvert L_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{1}}\lvert R_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{1}}\lvert L_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{2}}\lvert R_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{2}}

\displaystyle\mapsto\lvert y\rangle_{\mathsf{A}}\;\frac{1}{\sqrt{N^{|L_{2}|+|R_{2}|+3}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z})\in\mathsf{G}\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1},L_{2}\\
R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}\right)\colon\\
(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z})\notin{\mathcal{P}}_{y,L_{1},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}\end{subarray}}\lvert L^{(k_{1},k_{3})}_{1}\cup L_{2}^{(k_{2},\vec{z}_{L})}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}}\lvert R^{(k_{1},k_{3})}_{1}\cup R_{2}^{(k_{2},\vec{z}_{R})}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}}\lvert k\rangle_{\mathsf{K}}.

If there exists
\delta\geq 0
such that for any
\tau
,

\displaystyle\frac{\big|{\mathcal{P}}_{y,L_{1},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}\cap\mathsf{G}\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1},L_{2}\\
R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}\right)\big|}{N^{|L_{2}|+|R_{2}|+3}}\leq\delta,

then

\displaystyle\|{\mathcal{S}}^{\bullet}-{\mathcal{S}}\|_{\mathrm{op}}=\sqrt{\delta}.

Namely,
{\mathcal{S}}^{\bullet}
is further controlled by the register
\mathsf{A}
and
imposes extra conditions ensuring that good tuples
(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z})
do not lie in some “bad set”
{\mathcal{P}}_{\tau}
. We provide the proof in
Section

A.2
.

4.7
Main Lemmas

We introduce important lemmas regarding
{\mathcal{S}}
below. Their proofs are deferred to further subsections. We will first use these lemmas to prove
Lemma

4.8
in
Section

4.8
.

Lemma 4.36

(
{\mathcal{S}}
is Close to a Partial Isometry)
.

There exists a partial isometry
\widetilde{{\mathcal{S}}}
such that for any integer
t\geq 0
,

\|(\widetilde{{\mathcal{S}}}-{\mathcal{S}})\Pi_{\leq t}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq O(\sqrt{t^{2}/N}).

We provide the proof of above lemma in
Section

5
.

Lemma 4.37

(Closeness of the First Oracle)
.

For any integer
t\geq 0
,

•

Forward query:

\|(X^{k_{3}}FX^{k_{1}}{\mathcal{S}}-{\mathcal{S}}F_{1})\Pi_{\leq t}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq O(\sqrt{t/N})
,

•

Inverse query:

\|(X^{k_{1}}F^{\dagger}X^{k_{3}}{\mathcal{S}}-{\mathcal{S}}F_{1}^{\dagger})\Pi_{\leq t}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq O(\sqrt{t/N})
.

We provide the proof of above lemma in
Section

6
.

Lemma 4.38

(Closeness of the Second Oracle)
.

For any integer
t\geq 0
,

•

Forward query:

\|(FX^{k_{2}}F{\mathcal{S}}-{\mathcal{S}}F_{2})\Pi_{\leq t}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq O(t/\sqrt{N})
,

•

Inverse query:

\|(F^{\dagger}X^{k_{2}}F^{\dagger}{\mathcal{S}}-{\mathcal{S}}F_{2}^{\dagger})\Pi_{\leq t}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq O(t/\sqrt{N})
.

We provide the proof of above lemma in
Section

7
.

4.8
Statistical Closeness between
\mathbf{H}_{3}
and
\mathbf{H}_{4}
: Proving
Lemma

4.8

Now, we use the lemmas in
Section

4.7
to prove
Lemma

4.8
. The structure of the proof is similar to the commutator-style analysis in
[
CMS19
,
DFMS22
]
.

Proof of
Lemma

4.8
.

We first introduce some notations. Let
\lvert\psi_{0}\rangle
denote the initial state in hybrid
\mathbf{H}_{3}
, i.e.,

\lvert\psi_{0}\rangle\coloneqq\lvert 0\rangle_{\mathsf{A}}\lvert 0\rangle_{\mathsf{B}}\lvert\varnothing\rangle_{\mathsf{S_{1}}}\lvert\varnothing\rangle_{\mathsf{T_{1}}}\lvert\varnothing\rangle_{\mathsf{S_{2}}}\lvert\varnothing\rangle_{\mathsf{T_{2}}}.

For
i\in[4t]
, let
\lvert\psi_{i}\rangle
denote the state right after the
i
-th query,

\lvert\psi_{i}\rangle\coloneqq{\mathcal{O}}_{i}A_{i}\lvert\psi_{i-1}\rangle,

where
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
cycles through
F_{1},F_{2},F_{1}^{\dagger},F_{2}^{\dagger}
according to
i\bmod 4
. Similarly, denote the initial state in
\mathbf{H}_{4}
by

\lvert\phi_{0}\rangle\coloneqq\frac{1}{\sqrt{N^{3}}}\sum_{k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}\in[N]}\lvert 0\rangle_{\mathsf{A}}\lvert 0\rangle_{\mathsf{B}}\lvert\varnothing\rangle_{\mathsf{S}}\lvert\varnothing\rangle_{\mathsf{T}}\lvert k_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{K_{1}}}\lvert k_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{K_{2}}}\lvert k_{3}\rangle_{\mathsf{K_{3}}}.

For
i\in[4t]
, let
\lvert\psi_{i}\rangle
denote the state right after the
i
-th query,

\lvert\phi_{i}\rangle\coloneqq{\mathcal{O}}_{i}A_{i}\lvert\phi_{i-1}\rangle,

where
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
cycles through
X^{k_{3}}FX^{k_{1}},FX^{k_{2}}F,X^{k_{1}}F^{\dagger}X^{k_{3}},F^{\dagger}X^{k_{2}}F^{\dagger}
according to
i\bmod 4
.

Now, we prove that
\|{\mathcal{S}}\lvert\psi_{i}\rangle-\lvert\phi_{i}\rangle\|_{2}=O(i^{2}/\sqrt{N})
for
i\in[4t]
by induction.

Base case (
i=0
):

{\mathcal{S}}\lvert\psi_{0}\rangle=\lvert\phi_{0}\rangle
holds trivially.

Induction step:
Suppose
\|{\mathcal{S}}\lvert\psi_{i-1}\rangle-\lvert\phi_{i-1}\rangle\|_{2}=O((i-1)^{2}/\sqrt{N})
. Consider the following four cases:

Case 1.
i\equiv 1\bmod{4}
:

\displaystyle\|{\mathcal{S}}\lvert\psi_{i}\rangle-\lvert\phi_{i}\rangle\|_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\|{\mathcal{S}}F_{1}A_{i}\lvert\psi_{i-1}\rangle-X^{k_{3}}FX^{k_{1}}A_{i}\lvert\phi_{i-1}\rangle\|_{2}

(by expanding the definition of
\lvert\psi_{i}\rangle
and
\lvert\phi_{i}\rangle
)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|{\mathcal{S}}F_{1}A_{i}\lvert\psi_{i-1}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.72,0.1512,0.1512}-X^{k_{3}}FX^{k_{1}}A_{i}{\mathcal{S}}\lvert\psi_{i-1}\rangle}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 51.7479pt+\|{\color[rgb]{0.72,0.1512,0.1512}X^{k_{3}}FX^{k_{1}}A_{i}{\mathcal{S}}\lvert\psi_{i-1}\rangle}-X^{k_{3}}FX^{k_{1}}A_{i}\lvert\phi_{i-1}\rangle\|_{2}

(by the triangle inequality)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\|({\mathcal{S}}F_{i}-X^{k_{3}}FX^{k_{1}}{\mathcal{S}})A_{i}\lvert\psi_{i-1}\rangle\|_{2}+\|X^{k_{3}}FX^{k_{1}}A_{i}({\mathcal{S}}\lvert\psi_{i-1}\rangle-\lvert\phi_{i-1}\rangle)\|_{2}

(since
{\mathcal{S}}
and
A_{i}
commute)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|({\mathcal{S}}F_{1}-X^{k_{3}}FX^{k_{1}}{\mathcal{S}})\Pi_{\leq t}\|_{\mathrm{op}}+\norm{\Ospru\ket{\psi_{i-1}} - \ket{\phi_{i-1}}}_{2}

(by
Lemma

3.3
)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O(i^{2}/\sqrt{N}).

(by
Lemma

4.37
and the induction hypothesis)

Other three cases follow from the same argument. Hence, the induction holds true. In particular, when
i=4t
, we have

\|{\mathcal{S}}\lvert\psi_{4t}\rangle-\lvert\phi_{4t}\rangle\|_{2}=O(t^{2}/\sqrt{N}).

(22)

Let
\widetilde{{\mathcal{S}}}
be the partial isometry guaranteed to exist in
Lemma

4.36
. By the triangle inequality,
Lemma

4.36
, and
Equation

22
, we have

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{{\mathcal{S}}}\lvert\psi_{4t}\rangle-\lvert\phi_{4t}\rangle\|_{2}\leq\|\widetilde{{\mathcal{S}}}\lvert\psi_{4t}\rangle-{\mathcal{S}}\lvert\psi_{4t}\rangle\|_{2}+\|{\mathcal{S}}\lvert\psi_{4t}\rangle-\lvert\phi_{4t}\rangle\|_{2}=O\left(t^{2}/\sqrt{N}\right).

(23)

Finally, the trace distance between the output of
\mathbf{H}_{3}
and that of
\mathbf{H}_{4}
satisfies

\displaystyle\mathsf{TD}(\rho_{3},\rho_{4})

(24)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{TD}(\Tr_{\mathsf{S_{1}S_{2}T_{1}T_{2}}}(\lvert\psi_{4t}\rangle\!\langle\psi_{4t}\rvert),\Tr_{\mathsf{STK_{1}K_{2}K_{3}}}(\lvert\phi_{4t}\rangle\!\langle\phi_{4t}\rvert))

\displaystyle=\mathsf{TD}(\Tr_{\mathsf{STK_{1}K_{2}K_{3}}}(\widetilde{{\mathcal{S}}}\lvert\psi_{4t}\rangle\!\langle\psi_{4t}\rvert\widetilde{{\mathcal{S}}}^{\dagger}),\Tr_{\mathsf{STK_{1}K_{2}K_{3}}}(\lvert\phi_{4t}\rangle\!\langle\phi_{4t}\rvert))

(25)

\displaystyle\leq\mathsf{TD}(\widetilde{{\mathcal{S}}}\lvert\psi_{4t}\rangle\!\langle\psi_{4t}\rvert\widetilde{{\mathcal{S}}}^{\dagger},\lvert\phi_{4t}\rangle\!\langle\phi_{4t}\rvert)

(trace distance is non-increasing under partial trace)

\displaystyle\leq\|\widetilde{{\mathcal{S}}}\lvert\psi_{4t}\rangle-\lvert\phi_{4t}\rangle\|_{2}

(the trace distance between pure states is bounded by their Euclidean distance)

\displaystyle=O(t^{2}/\sqrt{N}),

(by
Equation

23
)

where
Equation

25
is because
\widetilde{{\mathcal{S}}}
is a partial isometry that acts on the registers being traced out, and
\lvert\psi_{4t}\rangle
is in the domain of
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}
. This completes the proof of
Lemma

4.8
.
∎

5
{\mathcal{S}}
is Close to a Partial Isometry: Proving
Lemma

4.36

We will define the “normalized” version of
{\mathcal{S}}_{k_{1},k_{3}},{\mathcal{S}}_{k_{2}},{\mathcal{S}}_{\vec{z}}
such that the coefficients match the number of terms in the sum. First, define the partial isometry
\widetilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{k_{1},k_{3}}
such that for any
L_{1},L_{2}\in{\mathcal{R}}^{{\mathcal{I}}\text{-}\mathrm{dist}},R_{1},R_{2}\in{\mathcal{R}}^{{\mathcal{D}}\text{-}\mathrm{dist}}
,

\displaystyle\widetilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{k_{1},k_{3}}\colon

\displaystyle\lvert L_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{1}}\lvert R_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{1}}\lvert L_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{2}}\lvert R_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{2}}

\displaystyle\mapsto\frac{1}{\sqrt{(N-\absolutevalue{\cB_1(L_1,L_2,R_1,R_2)})(\,N-\absolutevalue{\cB_3(L_1,L_2,R_1,R_2)}\,)}}

\displaystyle\hskip 68.99895pt\times\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}\notin{\mathcal{B}}_{1}(L_{1},L_{2},R_{1},R_{2})\\
k_{3}\notin{\mathcal{B}}_{3}(L_{1},L_{2},R_{1},R_{2})\end{subarray}}\lvert L_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{1}}\lvert R_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{1}}\lvert L_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{2}}\lvert R_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{2}}\lvert k_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{K}_{1}}\lvert k_{3}\rangle_{\mathsf{K}_{3}}\,.

Next, define the partial isometry
\widetilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{k_{2}}
such that for any
L_{1},L_{2}\in{\mathcal{R}}^{{\mathcal{I}}\text{-}\mathrm{dist}},R_{1},R_{2}\in{\mathcal{R}}^{{\mathcal{D}}\text{-}\mathrm{dist}},k_{1}\notin{\mathcal{B}}_{1}(L_{1},L_{2},

R_{1},R_{2}),k_{3}\notin{\mathcal{B}}_{3}(L_{1},L_{2},R_{1},R_{2})
,

\displaystyle\widetilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{k_{2}}\colon

\displaystyle\lvert L_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{1}}\lvert R_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{1}}\lvert L_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{2}}\lvert R_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{2}}\lvert k_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{K}_{1}}\lvert k_{3}\rangle_{\mathsf{K}_{3}}

\displaystyle\mapsto\frac{1}{\sqrt{N-\absolutevalue{\cB_2(L_1, L_2, R_1, R_2, k_1, k_3)}}}

\displaystyle\hskip 51.7479pt\times\sum_{k_{2}\notin{\mathcal{B}}_{2}(L_{1},L_{2},R_{1},R_{2},k_{1},k_{3})}\lvert L_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{1}}\lvert R_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{1}}\lvert L_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{2}}\lvert R_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{2}}\lvert k_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{K}_{1}}\lvert k_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{K}_{2}}\lvert k_{3}\rangle_{\mathsf{K}_{3}}\,.

Finally, define the partial isometry
\widetilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{\vec{z}}
such that for any
L_{1},L_{2}\in{\mathcal{R}}^{{\mathcal{I}}\text{-}\mathrm{dist}},R_{1},R_{2}\in{\mathcal{R}}^{{\mathcal{D}}\text{-}\mathrm{dist}},k_{1}\notin{\mathcal{B}}_{1}(L_{1},L_{2},

R_{1},R_{2}),k_{3}\notin{\mathcal{B}}_{3}(L_{1},L_{2},R_{1},R_{2}),k_{2}\notin{\mathcal{B}}_{2}(L_{1},L_{2},R_{1},R_{2},k_{1},k_{3})
,

\displaystyle\widetilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{\vec{z}}\colon

\displaystyle\lvert L_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{1}}\lvert R_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{1}}\lvert L_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{2}}\lvert R_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{2}}\lvert k_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{K}_{1}}\lvert k_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{K}_{2}}\lvert k_{3}\rangle_{\mathsf{K}_{3}}

\displaystyle\mapsto\frac{1}{\sqrt{N^{|L_{2}|}-\absolutevalue{\cB_L \left(\substack{L_1,L_2,\\ R_1,R_2,\\ k_1,k_2,k_3} \right)}}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{N^{|R_{2}|}-\absolutevalue{\cB_R \left(\substack{L_1,L_2,\\ R_1,R_2,\\ k_1,k_2,k_3} \right)}}}

\displaystyle\hskip 34.5021pt\times\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\vec{z}_{L}\notin{\mathcal{B}}_{L}\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1},L_{2},\\
R_{1},R_{2},\\
k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}\end{subarray}\right),\vec{z}_{R}\notin{\mathcal{B}}_{R}\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1},L_{2},\\
R_{1},R_{2},\\
k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}\end{subarray}\right)\end{subarray}}\lvert L_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{1}}\lvert R_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{1}}\lvert L_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{2}}\lvert R_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{2}}\lvert\vec{z}_{L}\rangle_{\mathsf{Z_{L}}}\lvert\vec{z}_{R}\rangle_{\mathsf{Z_{R}}}\lvert k_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{K}_{1}}\lvert k_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{K}_{2}}\lvert k_{3}\rangle_{\mathsf{K}_{3}}.

Lemma 5.1
.

For any interger
t\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\|({\mathcal{S}}_{k_{1},k_{3}}-\widetilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{k_{1},k_{3}})\Pi_{\leq t}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq O(\sqrt{t^{2}/N}),

\displaystyle\|({\mathcal{S}}_{k_{2}}-\widetilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{k_{2}})\Pi_{\leq t}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq O(\sqrt{t^{2}/N}),

\displaystyle\|({\mathcal{S}}_{\vec{z}}-\widetilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{\vec{z}})\Pi_{\leq t}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq O(\sqrt{t^{2}/N}).

Proof.

Since
{\mathcal{S}}_{k_{1},k_{3}}-\widetilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{k_{1},k_{3}}
preserve the orthogonality of input of the form
\lvert L_{1}\rangle\lvert R_{1}\rangle\lvert L_{2}\rangle\lvert R_{2}\rangle
. Thus, by
Lemma

3.4
, it is suffices to maximize

\displaystyle\|({\mathcal{S}}_{k_{1},k_{3}}-\widetilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{k_{1},k_{3}})\Pi_{\leq t}\lvert L_{1}\rangle\lvert R_{1}\rangle\lvert L_{2}\rangle\lvert R_{2}\rangle\|_{2}.

This follow from
Lemma

4.22
and an elementary calculation. Items 2 and 3 follow similarly.
∎

Proof of
Lemma

4.36
.

Define the operator
\widetilde{{\mathcal{S}}}\coloneqq{{\mathcal{D}}}^{\dagger}\cdot\widetilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{\vec{z}}\cdot\widetilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{k_{2}}\cdot\widetilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{k_{1},k_{3}}
. To see that
\widetilde{{\mathcal{S}}}
is a partial isometry, one can easily verify that
\widetilde{{\mathcal{S}}}
preserves the inner product between basis vectors in the domain.
16
16

16

In contrast to isometries, partial isometries are
not
necessarily closed under composition.
Then we obtain

\displaystyle\|({\mathcal{S}}-\widetilde{{\mathcal{S}}})\Pi_{\leq t}\|_{\mathrm{op}}

\displaystyle=\|{{\mathcal{D}}}^{\dagger}\cdot({\mathcal{S}}_{\vec{z}}\cdot{\mathcal{S}}_{k_{2}}\cdot{\mathcal{S}}_{k_{1},k_{3}}-\widetilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{\vec{z}}\cdot\widetilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{k_{2}}\cdot\widetilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{k_{1},k_{3}})\Pi_{\leq t}\|_{\mathrm{op}}

\displaystyle=\|({\mathcal{S}}_{\vec{z}}\cdot{\mathcal{S}}_{k_{2}}\cdot{\mathcal{S}}_{k_{1},k_{3}}-\widetilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{\vec{z}}\cdot\widetilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{k_{2}}\cdot\widetilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{k_{1},k_{3}})\Pi_{\leq t}\|_{\mathrm{op}}

(since
\|{{\mathcal{D}}}^{\dagger}\|_{\mathrm{op}}=\|{\mathcal{D}}\|_{\mathrm{op}}=1
)

\displaystyle\leq\|({\mathcal{S}}_{\vec{z}}-\widetilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{\vec{z}})\Pi_{\leq t}\|_{\mathrm{op}}+\|({\mathcal{S}}_{k_{2}}-\widetilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{k_{2}})\Pi_{\leq t}\|_{\mathrm{op}}+\|({\mathcal{S}}_{k_{1},k_{3}}-\widetilde{{\mathcal{S}}}_{k_{1},k_{3}})\Pi_{\leq t}\|_{\mathrm{op}}

(26)

\displaystyle=O(\sqrt{t^{2}/N}),

(by
Lemma

5.1
)

where
Equation

26
uses (i) the triangle inequality; (ii) that
\Pi_{\leq t}
commutes with each of
{\mathcal{S}}_{\vec{z}},{\mathcal{S}}_{k_{2}},{\mathcal{S}}_{k_{1},k_{3}}
; (iii) that the operator norm is submultiplicative; and (iv) that
{\mathcal{S}}_{\vec{z}},{\mathcal{S}}_{k_{2}},{\mathcal{S}}_{k_{1},k_{3}}
are partial isometries (so their operator norm is
1
). This completes the proof of
Lemma

4.36
.
∎

6
Closeness of the First Oracle: Proving
Lemma

4.37

Our approach is through expanding oracles
F_{1},F_{2},F
as a sum of smaller terms. Then we carefully bound each pair of terms. Before proving
Lemma

4.37
, we first introduce several lemmas.

6.1
Closeness of
F_{1}^{L}
and
F_{1}^{R}

Intuitively, the following lemma states the following. Suppose the adversary in hybrid
\mathbf{H}_{3}
has made
t=\mathsf{poly}({\lambda})
queries in total at the moment. Then the state obtained by applying
F_{1}^{L}
followed by
{\mathcal{S}}
is negligibly close to the state obtained by applying
{\mathcal{S}}
followed by
X^{k_{3}}F^{L}X^{k_{1}}
. The intuition is straightforward. If we apply
F_{1}^{L}
and then
{\mathcal{S}}
, the resulting state is entirely supported by decomposable relations with the correct number of correlated pairs, owing to the definition of
{\mathcal{S}}
. On the other hand, if we first apply
{\mathcal{S}}
, then
X^{k_{3}}F^{L}X^{k_{1}}
, there is a small chance that the
y
sampled by
F^{L}
might generate unwanted correlated pairs. Fortunately, since the relations are of polynomial size, all but a negligible fraction of
y
behave correctly.

Lemma 6.1

(Closeness of
F_{1}^{L}
and
F_{1}^{R}
)
.

For any integer
t\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\|(X^{k_{3}}F^{L}X^{k_{1}}{\mathcal{S}}-{\mathcal{S}}F_{1}^{L})\Pi_{\leq t}\|_{\mathrm{op}}=O(\sqrt{t/N})

\displaystyle\|(X^{k_{1}}F^{R}X^{k_{3}}{\mathcal{S}}-{\mathcal{S}}F_{1}^{R})\Pi_{\leq t}\|_{\mathrm{op}}=O(\sqrt{t/N}).

Proof.

Fix
t\in\mathbb{N},x\in[N],L_{1},L_{2}\in{\mathcal{R}}^{{\mathcal{I}}\text{-}\mathrm{dist}}_{\leq t}
, and
R_{1},R_{2}\in{\mathcal{R}}^{{\mathcal{D}}\text{-}\mathrm{dist}}_{\leq t}
. We start by calculating the following states:

\displaystyle\lvert\psi_{x,L_{1},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}\rangle_{\mathsf{AST}\mathsf{K}_{1}\mathsf{K}_{2}\mathsf{K}_{3}}\coloneqq X^{k_{3}}F^{L}X^{k_{1}}{\mathcal{S}}\lvert x\rangle_{\mathsf{A}}\lvert L_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{1}}\lvert R_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{1}}\lvert L_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{2}}\lvert R_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{2}},

\displaystyle\lvert\phi_{x,L_{1},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}\rangle_{\mathsf{AST}\mathsf{K}_{1}\mathsf{K}_{2}\mathsf{K}_{3}}\coloneqq{\mathcal{S}}F_{1}^{L}\lvert x\rangle_{\mathsf{A}}\lvert L_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{1}}\lvert R_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{1}}\lvert L_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{2}}\lvert R_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{2}}.

To simplify notation, we write
\lvert\mathbf{k}\rangle_{\mathsf{K}}
as shorthand for
\lvert k_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{K}_{1}}\lvert k_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{K}_{2}}\lvert k_{3}\rangle_{\mathsf{K}_{3}}
, and
(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z})\in\mathsf{G}\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1},L_{2},\\
R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}\right)
as shorthand for
(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},\vec{z}_{L},\vec{z}_{R})\in\mathsf{G}\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1},L_{2},\\
R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}\right)
.

Computing
\lvert\psi_{x,L_{1},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}\rangle
.
Expanding the definitions of
{\mathcal{S}}
and
F^{L}
, we have

\displaystyle\lvert x\rangle_{\mathsf{A}}\lvert L_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{1}}\lvert R_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{1}}\lvert L_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{2}}\lvert R_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{2}}

\displaystyle\xmapsto{{\mathcal{S}}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{N^{|L_{2}|+|R_{2}|+3}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z})\in\mathsf{G}\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1},L_{2},\\
R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}\right)\end{subarray}}\lvert x\rangle_{\mathsf{A}}\lvert L_{1}^{(k_{1},k_{3})}\cup L_{2}^{(k_{2},\vec{z}_{L})}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}}\lvert R^{(k_{1},k_{3})}_{1}\cup R_{2}^{(k_{2},\vec{z}_{R})}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}}\lvert\mathbf{k}\rangle_{\mathsf{K}}

(by
Lemma

4.34
)

\displaystyle\xmapsto{X^{k_{3}}F^{L}X^{k_{1}}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{N^{|L_{2}|+|R_{2}|+4}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z})\in\mathsf{G}\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1},L_{2},\\
R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}\right)\\
y\notin\imaginary(L_{1}^{(k_{1},k_{3})}\cup L_{2}^{(k_{2},\vec{z}_{L})})\end{subarray}}\lvert y\oplus k_{3}\rangle_{\mathsf{A}}\lvert L_{1}^{(k_{1},k_{3})}\cup L_{2}^{(k_{2},\vec{z}_{L})}\cup\{(x\oplus k_{1},y)\}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}}\lvert R^{(k_{1},k_{3})}_{1}\cup R_{2}^{(k_{2},\vec{z}_{R})}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}}\lvert\mathbf{k}\rangle_{\mathsf{K}}

(by
Equation

1
)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N^{|L_{2}|+|R_{2}|+4}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z})\in\mathsf{G}\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1},L_{2},\\
R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}\right)\\
y\oplus k_{3}\notin\imaginary(L_{1}^{(k_{1},k_{3})}\cup L_{2}^{(k_{2},\vec{z}_{L})})\end{subarray}}\lvert y\rangle_{\mathsf{A}}\lvert L_{1}^{(k_{1},k_{3})}\cup L_{2}^{(k_{2},\vec{z}_{L})}\cup\{(x\oplus k_{1},y\oplus k_{3})\}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}}\lvert R^{(k_{1},k_{3})}_{1}\cup R_{2}^{(k_{2},\vec{z}_{R})}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}}\lvert\mathbf{k}\rangle_{\mathsf{K}}

(by relabeling

)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N^{|L_{2}|+|R_{2}|+4}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z})\in\mathsf{G}\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1},L_{2},\\

(27)

where the last line is by the definition of augmented relations in

8
.

Computing
\lvert\phi_{x,L_{1},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}\rangle
.

Similarly, expanding the definitions of
{\mathcal{S}}
and
F_{1}^{L}
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{N^{|L_{2}|+|R_{2}|+4}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\notin\imaginary(L_{1}),\\

(28)

Orthogonality.

Consider distinct
(x,L_{1},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2})
and
(x^{\prime},L^{\prime}_{1},R^{\prime}_{1},L^{\prime}_{2},R^{\prime}_{2})
. We claim that

•

\lvert\psi_{x,L_{1},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}\rangle
is orthogonal to
\lvert\psi_{x^{\prime},L^{\prime}_{1},R^{\prime}_{1},L^{\prime}_{2},R^{\prime}_{2}}\rangle
,

•

\lvert\phi_{x,L_{1},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}\rangle
is orthogonal to
\lvert\phi_{x^{\prime},L^{\prime}_{1},R^{\prime}_{1},L^{\prime}_{2},R^{\prime}_{2}}\rangle
,

•

\lvert\psi_{x,L_{1},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}\rangle
is orthogonal to
\lvert\phi_{x^{\prime},L^{\prime}_{1},R^{\prime}_{1},L^{\prime}_{2},R^{\prime}_{2}}\rangle
.

They together implies that
\lvert\psi_{x,L_{1},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}\rangle-\lvert\phi_{x,L_{1},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}\rangle
is orthogonal to
\lvert\psi_{x^{\prime},L^{\prime}_{1},R^{\prime}_{1},L^{\prime}_{2},R^{\prime}_{2}}\rangle-\lvert\phi_{x^{\prime},L^{\prime}_{1},R^{\prime}_{1},L^{\prime}_{2},R^{\prime}_{2}}\rangle
. Thus, by
Lemma

3.4
, it suffices to maximize the norm over input states of the form
\lvert x\rangle\lvert L_{1}\rangle\lvert R_{1}\rangle\lvert L_{2}\rangle\lvert R_{2}\rangle
. To prove the claim, we define the operator

{\mathcal{I}}\coloneqq X^{k_{3}}\cdot X^{k_{1}}\cdot{\mathcal{D}}\cdot F_{\mathsf{extract}}^{L}\cdot X^{k_{3}}

where the partial isometry
F_{L}^{\mathsf{extract}}
is defined in
Equation

6
. Note that
{\mathcal{I}}
preserves inner product between the states under consideration. To see this, we may compute the states obtained by applying
{\mathcal{I}}
to them:

\displaystyle\lvert\psi_{x,L_{1},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}\rangle

\displaystyle\xmapsto{F_{\mathsf{extract}}^{L}\cdot X^{k_{3}}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{N^{|L_{2}|+|R_{2}|+4}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z})\in\mathsf{G}\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1},L_{2},\\
R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}\right)\\
y\notin\imaginary(L_{1})\cup\quantity( \Im(L_2^{(k_2,\vec{z}_L)}) \oplus k_3 )\end{subarray}}\lvert y\oplus k_{3}\rangle_{\mathsf{A}^{\prime}}\lvert x\oplus k_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{A}}\lvert L_{1}^{(k_{1},k_{3})}\cup L_{2}^{(k_{2},\vec{z}_{L})}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}}\lvert R^{(k_{1},k_{3})}_{1}\cup R_{2}^{(k_{2},\vec{z}_{R})}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}}\lvert\mathbf{k}\rangle_{\mathsf{K}}

(by
Equations

27
and
6
)

\displaystyle\xmapsto{X^{k_{3}}\cdot X^{k_{1}}\cdot{\mathcal{D}}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{N^{|L_{2}|+|R_{2}|+4}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z})\in\mathsf{G}\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1},L_{2},\\
R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}\right)\\
y\notin\imaginary(L_{1})\cup\quantity( \Im(L_2^{(k_2,\vec{z}_L)}) \oplus k_3 )\end{subarray}}\lvert y\rangle_{\mathsf{A}^{\prime}}\lvert x\rangle_{\mathsf{A}}\lvert L_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{1}}\lvert L_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{2}}\lvert R_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{1}}\lvert R_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{2}}\lvert\mathbf{z}\rangle_{\mathsf{Z}}\lvert\mathbf{k}\rangle_{\mathsf{K}},

(29)

where the last line is by
Definition

4.13
. From the above calculation, it is clear that
{\mathcal{I}}\lvert\psi_{x,L_{1},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}\rangle
is orthogonal to
{\mathcal{I}}\lvert\psi_{x^{\prime},L^{\prime}_{1},R^{\prime}_{1},L^{\prime}_{2},R^{\prime}_{2}}\rangle
whenever
(x,L_{1},L_{2},{R}_{1},{R}_{2})\neq(x^{\prime},L^{\prime}_{1},L^{\prime}_{2},{R^{\prime}}_{1},{R^{\prime}}_{2})
. Moreover,
X^{k_{3}}\lvert\psi_{x,L_{1},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}\rangle
is in the domain of the partial isometry
F_{L}^{\mathsf{extract}}
, and
F_{L}^{\mathsf{extract}}X^{k_{3}}\lvert\psi_{x,L_{1},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}\rangle
is in the domain of the partial isometry
{\mathcal{D}}
. Thus,
{\mathcal{I}}
preserves the inner product between
\lvert\psi_{x,L_{1},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}\rangle
and
\lvert\psi_{x^{\prime},L^{\prime}_{1},R^{\prime}_{1},L^{\prime}_{2},R^{\prime}_{2}}\rangle
, which implies Item 1, namely,
\lvert\psi_{x,L_{1},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}\rangle
is orthogonal to
\lvert\psi_{x^{\prime},L^{\prime}_{1},R^{\prime}_{1},L^{\prime}_{2},R^{\prime}_{2}}\rangle
.

Similarly, we have

\displaystyle\lvert\phi_{x,L_{1},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}\rangle\xmapsto{{\mathcal{I}}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{N^{|L_{2}|+|R_{2}|+4}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\notin\imaginary(L_{1}),\\
(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z})\in\mathsf{G}\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1}\cup\{(x,y)\},L_{2},\\
R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}\right)\end{subarray}}\lvert y\rangle_{\mathsf{A}^{\prime}}\lvert x\rangle_{\mathsf{A}}\lvert L_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{1}}\lvert L_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{2}}\lvert R_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{1}}\lvert R_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{2}}\lvert\mathbf{z}\rangle_{\mathsf{Z}}\lvert\mathbf{k}\rangle_{\mathsf{K}}.

(30)

Likewise,
{\mathcal{I}}\lvert\phi_{x,L_{1},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}\rangle
is orthogonal to
{\mathcal{I}}\lvert\phi_{x^{\prime},L^{\prime}_{1},R^{\prime}_{1},L^{\prime}_{2},R^{\prime}_{2}}\rangle
and
{\mathcal{I}}
preserves the inner product between
\lvert\phi_{x,L_{1},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}\rangle
and
\lvert\phi_{x^{\prime},L^{\prime}_{1},R^{\prime}_{1},L^{\prime}_{2},R^{\prime}_{2}}\rangle
. Thus,
\lvert\phi_{x,L_{1},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}\rangle
is orthogonal to
\lvert\phi_{x^{\prime},L^{\prime}_{1},R^{\prime}_{1},L^{\prime}_{2},R^{\prime}_{2}}\rangle
, proving Item 2. Finally, from the above calculation, we can easily conclude that
{\mathcal{I}}\lvert\psi_{x,L_{1},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}\rangle
is orthogonal to
\lvert\phi_{x^{\prime},L^{\prime}_{1},R^{\prime}_{1},L^{\prime}_{2},R^{\prime}_{2}}\rangle

which imply that
\lvert\psi_{x,L_{1},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}\rangle
is orthogonal to
\lvert\phi_{x^{\prime},L^{\prime}_{1},R^{\prime}_{1},L^{\prime}_{2},R^{\prime}_{2}}\rangle
, proving Item 3.

Wrap-up.

According to the above argument and
Lemma

3.4
, it suffices to bound the maximum of

\|\lvert\psi_{x,L_{1},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}\rangle-\lvert\phi_{x,L_{1},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}\rangle\|_{2}.

over all
x\in[N],L_{1},L_{2}\in{\mathcal{R}}^{{\mathcal{I}}\text{-}\mathrm{dist}}_{\leq t},R_{1},R_{2}\in{\mathcal{R}}^{{\mathcal{D}}\text{-}\mathrm{dist}}_{\leq t}
. From the above calculation, this is equivalently reduced to bounding

\|{\mathcal{I}}\lvert\psi_{x,L_{1},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}\rangle-{\mathcal{I}}\lvert\phi_{x,L_{1},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}\rangle\|_{2}.

Finally, by
Lemma

4.26
, we obtain

\displaystyle\|{\mathcal{I}}\lvert\psi_{x,L_{1},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}\rangle-{\mathcal{I}}\lvert\phi_{x,L_{1},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}\rangle\|^{2}_{2}=O(t/N).

This concludes the proof of
Lemma

6.1
.
∎

6.2
Closeness of
F_{1}^{L,\dagger}
and
F_{1}^{R,\dagger}

The following lemma implies that in
\mathbf{H}_{3}
, any state orthogonal to the image of
F_{1}^{L}
remains nearly orthogonal to the image of
F^{L}
after the action of
X^{k_{3}}{\mathcal{S}}
. Intuitively, this prevents unintended “cancellation” between oracle calls to
F^{L}
.

Lemma 6.2

(Image Lemma for
F_{1}^{L}
)
.

For any integer
t\geq 0
and any normalized state
\lvert\psi\rangle
on registers
\mathsf{A},\mathsf{B},\mathsf{S}_{1},\mathsf{T}_{1},\mathsf{S}_{2},\mathsf{T}_{2}
such that
\Pi_{\leq t}\lvert\psi\rangle=\lvert\psi\rangle
and
F_{1}^{L,\dagger}\lvert\psi\rangle=0
, it holds that

\|F^{L,\dagger}X^{k_{3}}{\mathcal{S}}\lvert\psi\rangle\|_{2}=O(\sqrt{t/N}).

Proof.

Suppose
\lvert\psi\rangle
can be written as

\lvert\psi\rangle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y,b\\
L_{1},L_{2},R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}}\alpha_{y,b,L_{1},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}\lvert y\rangle_{\mathsf{A}}\lvert b\rangle_{\mathsf{B}}\lvert L_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{1}}\lvert R_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{1}}\lvert L_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{2}}\lvert R_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{2}},

where
y\in[N]
,
L_{1},L_{2}\in{\mathcal{R}}^{{\mathcal{I}}\text{-}\mathrm{dist}}_{\leq t}
and
R_{1},R_{2}\in{\mathcal{R}}^{{\mathcal{D}}\text{-}\mathrm{dist}}_{\leq t}
; recall that
\mathsf{B}
is the adversary’s auxiliary register, and
b
ranges from some finite set that we do not explicitly specify.

Zero condition.
The premise implies that

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=F_{1}^{L,\dagger}\cdot\lvert\psi\rangle

\displaystyle=F_{1}^{L,\dagger}\cdot\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y,b\\
L_{1},L_{2},R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}}\alpha_{y,b,L_{1},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}\lvert y\rangle_{\mathsf{A}}\lvert b\rangle_{\mathsf{B}}\lvert L_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{1}}\lvert R_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{1}}\lvert L_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{2}}\lvert R_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}b,L_{1},L_{2},R_{1},R_{2}\\
(x,y)\in L_{1}\end{subarray}}\alpha_{y,b,L_{1},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}\lvert x\rangle_{\mathsf{A}}\lvert b\rangle_{\mathsf{B}}\lvert L_{1}\setminus\{(x,y)\}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{1}}\lvert R_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{1}}\lvert L_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{2}}\lvert R_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{2}}.

(by
Equation

4
)

By re-writing
L_{1}=L^{\prime}_{1}\cup\{(x,y)\}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,b\\
L^{\prime}_{1},L_{2},R_{1},R_{2}\\
y\notin\imaginary(L^{\prime}_{1})\end{subarray}}\alpha_{y,b,L^{\prime}_{1}\cup\{(x,y)\},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}\lvert x\rangle_{\mathsf{A}}\lvert b\rangle_{\mathsf{B}}\lvert L^{\prime}_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{1}}\lvert R_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{1}}\lvert L_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{2}}\lvert R_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,b\\
L^{\prime}_{1},L_{2},R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\notin\imaginary(L^{\prime}_{1})\end{subarray}}\alpha_{y,b,L^{\prime}_{1}\cup\{(x,y)\},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}\right)\lvert x\rangle_{\mathsf{A}}\lvert b\rangle_{\mathsf{B}}\lvert L^{\prime}_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{1}}\lvert R_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{1}}\lvert L_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{2}}\lvert R_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{2}},

where
x\in[N]
and
L^{\prime}_{1}\in{\mathcal{R}}^{{\mathcal{I}}\text{-}\mathrm{dist}}_{\leq t-1}
. Therefore, for any fixed
x\in[N]
,
b
,
L^{\prime}_{1}\in{\mathcal{R}}^{{\mathcal{I}}\text{-}\mathrm{dist}}_{\leq t-1},L_{2}\in{\mathcal{R}}^{{\mathcal{I}}\text{-}\mathrm{dist}}_{\leq t}
, and
R_{1},R_{2}\in{\mathcal{R}}^{{\mathcal{D}}\text{-}\mathrm{dist}}_{\leq t}
, it holds that

\sum_{y\notin\imaginary(L^{\prime}_{1})}\alpha_{y,b,L^{\prime}_{1}\cup\{(x,y)\},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}=0.

(31)

Computing
F^{L,\dagger}X^{k_{3}}{\mathcal{S}}\lvert\psi\rangle
.
Next, we will compute
F^{L,\dagger}X^{k_{3}}{\mathcal{S}}\lvert\psi\rangle
. Firstly, by
Lemma

4.34
, we obtain

\displaystyle\lvert\psi\rangle\xmapsto{X^{k_{3}}{\mathcal{S}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y,b\\
L_{1},L_{2},R_{1},R_{2}\\
(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z})\in\mathsf{G}\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1},L_{2}\\
R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}\right)\end{subarray}}\frac{\alpha_{y,b,L_{1},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}}{\sqrt{N^{|L_{2}|+|R_{2}|+3}}}\lvert y\oplus k_{3}\rangle_{\mathsf{A}}\lvert b\rangle_{\mathsf{B}}\lvert L^{(k_{1},k_{3})}_{1}\cup L^{(k_{2},\vec{z}_{L})}_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}}\lvert R^{(k_{1},k_{3})}_{1}\cup R^{(k_{2},\vec{z}_{R})}_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}}\lvert\mathbf{k}\rangle_{\mathsf{K}}.

Before we move on to apply
F^{L,\dagger}
, recall
Equation

4
. For the expression to be nonzero, it is necessary that
y\oplus k_{3}\in L^{(k_{1},k_{3})}_{1}\cup L^{(k_{2},\vec{z}_{L})}_{2}
. By
Lemma

4.20
,
L^{(k_{1},k_{3})}_{1}
and
L^{(k_{2},\vec{z}_{L})}_{2}
are disjoint. Therefore,
(x,y\oplus k_{3})
must belong to exactly one of the following: (i)
(x,y\oplus k_{3})\in L^{(k_{1},k_{3})}_{1}
, (ii)
(x,y\oplus k_{3})\in L^{(k_{2},\vec{z}_{L})}_{2}
. Define two projectors

\displaystyle\Pi_{1}\coloneqq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(L,\mathbf{k})\colon G^{{\mathcal{I}}}_{L,k_{2}}\text{ is decomposable}\\
y\in\imaginary(V_{\sf isolate}(G^{\ell}_{L,k_{2}}))\end{subarray}}\lvert y\rangle\!\langle y\rvert_{\mathsf{A}}\otimes\lvert L\rangle\!\langle L\rvert_{\mathsf{S}}\otimes\lvert\mathbf{k}\rangle\!\langle\mathbf{k}\rvert_{\mathsf{K}},

\displaystyle\Pi_{2}\coloneqq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(L,\mathbf{k})\colon G^{\ell}_{L,k_{2}}\text{ is decomposable}\\
y\in\imaginary(V_{\sf target}(G^{\ell}_{L,k_{2}})\cup V_{\sf source}(G^{\ell}_{L,k_{2}}))\end{subarray}}\lvert y\rangle\!\langle y\rvert_{\mathsf{A}}\otimes\lvert L\rangle\!\langle L\rvert_{\mathsf{S}}\otimes\lvert\mathbf{k}\rangle\!\langle\mathbf{k}\rvert_{\mathsf{K}}

Thus, it holds that

\displaystyle F^{L,\dagger}X^{k_{3}}{\mathcal{S}}\lvert\psi\rangle=F^{L,\dagger}\Pi_{1}X^{k_{3}}{\mathcal{S}}\lvert\psi\rangle+F^{L,\dagger}\Pi_{2}X^{k_{3}}{\mathcal{S}}\lvert\psi\rangle.

By the triangle inequality, it suffices to the bound the norm of each term.

Bounding
F^{L,\dagger}\Pi_{1}X^{k_{3}}{\mathcal{S}}\lvert\psi\rangle
.

Using
Equation

4
, we obtain

\displaystyle F^{L,\dagger}\Pi_{1}X^{k_{3}}{\mathcal{S}}\lvert\psi\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}b,L_{1},L_{2},R_{1},R_{2}\\
(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z})\in\mathsf{G}\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1},L_{2}\\
R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}\right)\\
x,y\colon(x,y\oplus k_{3})\in L_{1}^{(k_{1},k_{3})}\end{subarray}}\frac{\alpha_{y,b,L_{1},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}}{\sqrt{N^{|L_{2}|+|R_{2}|+4}}}\lvert x\rangle_{\mathsf{A}}\lvert b\rangle_{\mathsf{B}}\lvert L^{(k_{1},k_{3})}_{1}\cup L^{(k_{2},\vec{z}_{L})}_{2}\setminus\{(x,y\oplus k_{3})\}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}}\lvert R^{(k_{1},k_{3})}_{1}\cup R^{(k_{2},\vec{z}_{R})}_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}}\lvert\mathbf{k}\rangle_{\mathsf{K}}.

Crucially, we use
Lemma

4.18
to conclude that
V_{\sf isolate}\left(G^{\ell}_{L^{(k_{1},k_{3})}_{1}\cup L^{(k_{2},\vec{z}_{L})}_{2},k_{2}}\right)=L^{(k_{1},k_{3})}_{1}
. Thus, we are summing over elements in
L^{(k_{1},k_{3})}_{1}
in the above line.

By substituting
x=x^{\prime}\oplus k_{1}
and using that
(\hat{x}\oplus k_{1},\hat{y}\oplus k_{3})\in L^{(k_{1},k_{3})}_{1}
if and only if
(\hat{x},\hat{y})\in L_{1}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}b,L_{1},L_{2},R_{1},R_{2}\\
(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z})\in\mathsf{G}\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1},L_{2}\\
R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}\right)\\
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0.1512,0.1512}(x^{\prime},y)\in L_{1}}\end{subarray}}\frac{\alpha_{y,b,L_{1},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}}{\sqrt{N^{|L_{2}|+|R_{2}|+4}}}\lvert{\color[rgb]{0.72,0.1512,0.1512}x^{\prime}\oplus k_{1}}\rangle_{\mathsf{A}}\lvert b\rangle_{\mathsf{B}}\lvert({\color[rgb]{0.72,0.1512,0.1512}L_{1}\setminus\{(x^{\prime},y)\}})^{(k_{1},k_{3})}\cup L^{(k_{2},\vec{z}_{L})}_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}}\lvert R^{(k_{1},k_{3})}_{1}\cup R^{(k_{2},\vec{z}_{R})}_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}}\lvert\mathbf{k}\rangle_{\mathsf{K}}.

Substituting
L_{1}=L^{\prime}_{1}\cup\{(x^{\prime},y)\}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}b,{\color[rgb]{0.72,0.1512,0.1512}L^{\prime}_{1}},L_{2},R_{1},R_{2}\\
x^{\prime},{\color[rgb]{0.72,0.1512,0.1512}y\notin\imaginary(L^{\prime}_{1})}\\
(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z})\in\mathsf{G}\left(\begin{subarray}{c}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0.1512,0.1512}L^{\prime}_{1}\cup\{(x^{\prime},y)\}},L_{2}\\
R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}\right)\end{subarray}}\frac{\alpha_{y,b,{\color[rgb]{0.72,0.1512,0.1512}L^{\prime}_{1}\cup\{(x^{\prime},y)\}},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}}{\sqrt{N^{|L_{2}|+|R_{2}|+4}}}\lvert x^{\prime}\oplus k_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{A}}\lvert b\rangle_{\mathsf{B}}\lvert({\color[rgb]{0.72,0.1512,0.1512}L^{\prime}_{1}})^{(k_{1},k_{3})}\cup L^{(k_{2},\vec{z}_{L})}_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}}\lvert R^{(k_{1},k_{3})}_{1}\cup R^{(k_{2},\vec{z}_{R})}_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}}\lvert\mathbf{k}\rangle_{\mathsf{K}}.

(32)

We will further simplify the state. By monotonicity (
Lemma

4.23
), we can see that
Equation

32
is in the domain of the partial isometry
{\mathcal{D}}
. Thus, we can equivalently evaluate the norm of
{\mathcal{D}}\cdot X^{k_{1}}\cdot F^{L,\dagger}\Pi_{1}X^{k_{3}}{\mathcal{S}}\lvert\psi\rangle
. Thus, applying
{\mathcal{D}}\cdot X^{k_{1}}
to
Equation

32
, we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}b,L^{\prime}_{1},L_{2},R_{1},R_{2}\\
x^{\prime},y\notin\imaginary(L^{\prime}_{1})\\
(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z})\in\mathsf{G}\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L^{\prime}_{1}\cup\{(x^{\prime},y)\},L_{2}\\
R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}\right)\end{subarray}}\frac{\alpha_{y,b,L^{\prime}_{1}\cup\{(x^{\prime},y)\},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}}{\sqrt{N^{|L_{2}|+|R_{2}|+4}}}\lvert{\color[rgb]{0.72,0.1512,0.1512}x^{\prime}}\rangle_{\mathsf{A}}\lvert b\rangle_{\mathsf{B}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0.1512,0.1512}\lvert L^{\prime}_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{1}}\lvert R_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{1}}\lvert L_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{2}}\lvert R_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{2}}\lvert\mathbf{z}\rangle_{\mathsf{Z}}}\lvert\mathbf{k}\rangle_{\mathsf{K}}.

Rearranging, we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}b,L^{\prime}_{1},L_{2},R_{1},R_{2},x^{\prime},\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z}\\
y\notin\imaginary(L^{\prime}_{1})\colon(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z})\in\mathsf{G}\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L^{\prime}_{1}\cup\{(x^{\prime},y)\},L_{2}\\
R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}\right)\end{subarray}}\frac{\alpha_{y,b,L^{\prime}_{1}\cup\{(x^{\prime},y)\},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}}{\sqrt{N^{|L_{2}|+|R_{2}|+4}}}\lvert x^{\prime}\rangle_{\mathsf{A}}\lvert b\rangle_{\mathsf{B}}\lvert L^{\prime}_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{1}}\lvert L_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{2}}\lvert R_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{1}}\lvert R_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{2}}\lvert\mathbf{z}\rangle_{\mathsf{Z}}\lvert\mathbf{k}\rangle_{\mathsf{K}},

(33)

where
x^{\prime}\in[N],k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}\in[N],\vec{z}_{L}\in[N]^{|L_{2}|}_{\mathrm{dist}},\vec{z}_{R}\in[N]^{|R_{2}|}_{\mathrm{dist}}
and we range over
y
at the end.

The squared norm of
Equation

33
is

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}b,L^{\prime}_{1},L_{2},R_{1},R_{2},x^{\prime}\\
\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z}\end{subarray}}\Bigl|\sum_{y\notin\imaginary(L^{\prime}_{1})\colon(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z})\in\mathsf{G}\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L^{\prime}_{1}\cup\{(x^{\prime},y)\},L_{2}\\
R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}\right)}\frac{\alpha_{y,b,L^{\prime}_{1}\cup\{(x^{\prime},y)\},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}}{\sqrt{N^{|L_{2}|+|R_{2}|+4}}}\Bigr|^{2}.

By
Lemma

4.25
, once
(L^{\prime}_{1},L_{2},R_{1},R_{2},x^{\prime},\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z})
is fixed, there is either zero or at least
N-g(t)
values of
y
that satisfy the condition, where
g(t)=O(t)
is some function guaranteed by
Lemma

4.25
. Let
\mathsf{BAD}
denote the set of tuples for which the latter case holds. We obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{b,{\color[rgb]{0.72,0.1512,0.1512}(L^{\prime}_{1},L_{2},R_{1},R_{2},x^{\prime},\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z})\in\mathsf{BAD}}}\Bigl|\sum_{y\notin\imaginary(L^{\prime}_{1})\colon(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z})\in\mathsf{G}\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L^{\prime}_{1}\cup\{(x^{\prime},y)\},L_{2}\\
R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}\right)}\frac{\alpha_{y,b,L^{\prime}_{1}\cup\{(x^{\prime},y)\},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}}{\sqrt{N^{|L_{2}|+|R_{2}|+4}}}\Bigr|^{2}.

Now, we make crucial use of the condition implied by the premise (
Equation

31
) to obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{b,(L^{\prime}_{1},L_{2},R_{1},R_{2},x^{\prime},\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z})\in\mathsf{BAD}}\Bigl|{\color[rgb]{0.72,0.1512,0.1512}-}\sum_{y\notin\imaginary(L^{\prime}_{1})\colon(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z}){\color[rgb]{0.72,0.1512,0.1512}\notin}\mathsf{G}\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L^{\prime}_{1}\cup\{(x^{\prime},y)\},L_{2}\\
R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}\right)}\frac{\alpha_{y,b,L^{\prime}_{1}\cup\{(x^{\prime},y)\},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}}{\sqrt{N^{|L_{2}|+|R_{2}|+4}}}\Bigr|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{b,(L^{\prime}_{1},L_{2},R_{1},R_{2},x^{\prime},\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z})\in\mathsf{BAD}}\frac{1}{N^{|L_{2}|+|R_{2}|+4}}\cdot\Bigl|\sum_{y\notin\imaginary(L^{\prime}_{1})\colon(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z})\notin\mathsf{G}\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L^{\prime}_{1}\cup\{(x^{\prime},y)\},L_{2}\\
R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}\right)}\alpha_{y,b,L^{\prime}_{1}\cup\{(x^{\prime},y)\},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}\Bigr|^{2}.

(34)

Using the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and the definition of
\mathsf{BAD}
to bound the number of
y
in the sum, we can bound
Section

6.2
by

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}b,(L^{\prime}_{1},L_{2},R_{1},R_{2},x^{\prime},\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z})\in\mathsf{BAD}\\
y\notin\imaginary(L^{\prime}_{1})\colon(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z})\notin\mathsf{G}\left(\begin{subarray}{c}L^{\prime}_{1}\cup\{(x^{\prime},y)\},L_{2}\\
R_{1},R_{2}\end{subarray}\right)\end{subarray}}\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0.1512,0.1512}g(t)}}{N^{|L_{2}|+|R_{2}|+4}}\cdot\Bigl|\alpha_{y,b,L^{\prime}_{1}\cup\{(x^{\prime},y)\},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}\Bigr|^{2},

Since we are summing over non-negative terms, by relaxing the constraints, we can bound it by

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}b,{\color[rgb]{0.72,0.1512,0.1512}L^{\prime}_{1},L_{2},R_{1},R_{2},x^{\prime},\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0.1512,0.1512}y\notin\imaginary(L^{\prime}_{1})}\end{subarray}}\frac{g(t)}{N^{|L_{2}|+|R_{2}|+4}}\cdot\Bigl|\alpha_{y,b,L^{\prime}_{1}\cup\{(x^{\prime},y)\},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}\Bigr|^{2}.

By summing over
(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z})
, and noting that there are at most
N^{|L_{2}|+|R_{2}|+3}
such tuples, we can bounded it by

\displaystyle\frac{g(t)}{N}\cdot\sum_{b,L^{\prime}_{1},L_{2},R_{1},R_{2},x^{\prime},y\notin\imaginary(L^{\prime}_{1})}\Bigl|\alpha_{y,b,L^{\prime}_{1}\cup\{(x^{\prime},y)\},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}\Bigr|^{2}.

By substituting
L=L^{\prime}_{1}\cup\{(x^{\prime},y)\}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{g(t)}{N}\cdot\sum_{b,{\color[rgb]{0.72,0.1512,0.1512}L_{1}},L_{2},R_{1},R_{2},{\color[rgb]{0.72,0.1512,0.1512}y\in\imaginary(L_{1})}}\Bigl|\alpha_{y,b,{\color[rgb]{0.72,0.1512,0.1512}L_{1}},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}\Bigr|^{2}=O(t/N)

by the normalization condition of
\lvert\psi\rangle
.

Bounding
F^{L,\dagger}\Pi_{2}X^{k_{3}}{\mathcal{S}}\lvert\psi\rangle
.

Recall
Lemma

4.35
. For any
(y,L_{1},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2})
, define the set

\displaystyle{\mathcal{P}}_{y,L_{1},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}:=\{(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{z})\colon y\oplus k_{3}\in\imaginary(L_{2}^{(k_{2},\vec{z}_{L})})\}.

By sampling
k_{3}
at the end and the union bound, it is clear that
{\mathcal{P}}_{y,L_{1},R_{1},L_{2},R_{2}}
occupies at most a
2t/N
fraction of its universe. Thus, we obtain

\displaystyle\|F^{L,\dagger}\Pi_{2}X^{k_{3}}{\mathcal{S}}\lvert\psi\rangle\|_{2}
